#2879. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2878. Analysis: The Democrats have one chance to extract concessions from Trump. Markets will hate it

The electoral sweep by Democrats on Tuesday night injects a new, unstable dynamic into the single biggest political risk to financial markets in the coming year: the debt limit. Unless a defeated Republican Party makes a swift and unlikely show of unity to take the issue off the table in its last few weeks in power, the protracted fight that will unfold is set to be nastier than ever, adding volatility to an already shaky bond market.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

Past episodes of brinkmanship around the need to raise the government's borrowing limit have involved Republicans in Congress demanding budget cuts from a Democratic president as the price of their vote. This time it will almost certainly be the left seeking concessions from the right.

The debt limit is the definition of a must-pass bill, even more than issues like funding the government and the defense budget. That makes it potentially the one real chance the new Democratic majority in Congress has to extract concessions from a president who is already pledging to veto their agenda. Markets will hate the entire process, even if — as many expect — it ends in compromise.

Because it is Democrats who hold the leverage, unlike past episodes, the ultimate agreement is more likely to increase government spending than lower it. With high bond yields weighing on the economy, and Americans expecting action from a newly empowered Democratic opposition, the fight could be particularly damaging.

Many in the markets are stuck on the outdated idea that Democrats are less assertive than Republicans, said Aniket Shah, head of the Washington policy team at the investment bank Jefferies.

"They have this sort of mental image of Biden-Harris 2024," said Shah. "I don't think they realize how aggressively Democrats plan to take 2027."

The debt ceiling is a quirk of the congressional budgeting process. It stops the government from borrowing funds that Congress has already ordered federal agencies to spend. And because Congress has run deficits for decades — this year at $1.99 trillion — the pile of debt periodically bumps into the debt ceiling.

Congress last set the limit in 2025, when it gave the Treasury Department the authority to borrow $41.1 trillion. The Treasury is likely to hit its maximum borrowing capacity by midsummer, according to the Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonprofit that tracks the issue.

The Treasury can use budgeting techniques to extend the so-called X-date, when the U.S. would no longer be able to meet all its obligations, to sometime in the fourth quarter of 2027, according to Wall Street estimates.

A few technical hiccups aside, the U.S. has never meaningfully breached the debt limit. The results of the U.S. missing payments would be chaotic as damage ripples out across the financial system into the global economy. U.S. default would immediately call into question trillions in Treasury securities that are the lifeblood of the global financial system. Damaging them could send the U.S. and global economies into an economic tailspin with no obvious means of recovery.

Because the stakes are so high, Shah and many others on Wall Street have come to believe that the episode will produce volatility but will ultimately be resolved in compromise.

Fights under former Presidents Barack Obama and Joe Biden saw Republican congressional majorities use the issue to ram through spending reductions. (The 2025 raise was less contentious, because Republicans held Congress and the presidency. It was passed along party lines.)

Still, the risk-taking in those episodes contributed to ratings firms' decisions to downgrade U.S. debt — and could again.

President Donald Trump has called for Republicans to resolve the issue in the next few weeks, before the new Democratic-majority Congress is seated.

That isn't likely. The 2025 debt limit increase only passed because it was tied to a package of tax cuts and other policy changes. Republican debt hawks complained but held their noses.

Republicans may attempt to raise the limit in the coming lame-duck session under a budgeting procedure called reconciliation, which would allow them to move ahead without Democrats' votes. But success would require unity among a fractured, fleeting Republican majority that also faces several other more pressing priorities.

The most likely path toward raising the debt limit is the incoming Democratic majority negotiating another temporary fix with Trump. But they aren't going to make it easy. The president has occasionally made noises about making deals, but the political incentives ahead of the 2028 presidential election are for both sides to dig in.

"[Democrats] are going to want to embarrass the White House, and they're going to want to get leverage," Shah said. Democrats will use that to push to reverse cuts to Medicaid spending passed in 2025 and to try to restore healthcare spending, he said.

Democrats could also seek to tie the debt limit to restraining signature Trump policies, such as limiting the war with Iran or rolling back tariffs, analysts at Goldman Sachs said in a research note Wednesday.

Another potential compromise being considered in some corners of Washington is for Democrats to tie an increase in the corporate tax rate to a debt limit raise, said George Pollack, senior U.S. analyst with Signum Global Advisors. Trump has shown he can be surprisingly flexible on similar issues, Pollack noted.

That is what will make this debt limit fight different. In the past, Republicans used it to try to win spending reductions they couldn't get through regular budget negotiations. Now Democrats will broaden it out into an all-purpose hostage. That effort is far more likely to raise overall government spending than cut it. "There's a very weird irony here," said Robert Sockin, chief U.S. economist at PGIM, an asset manager.

The path to compromise will likely be rocky. Sockin believes it may turn out like the 2025 government shutdown. That was the longest government-wide shutdown in history, even though Democrats weren't in control of either chamber of Congress. This time they will have even more power.

If the fight extends beyond the X-date, missing debt payments could do serious damage to the financial system.

"It's not our base case, but we think the risk is higher than ever that you could get a technical default from the government," Sockin said. That would see the U.S. miss a payment, even if the government eventually makes it up.

That would still hurt. "You'll get more downgrades to the U.S. sovereign, and those might bite more this time," Sockin said. Yields on longer-term Treasurys would rise to account for added uncertainty.

Crossing the red line could prompt a "very negative reaction in equity markets," he said.

The coming months will show just how close Democrats are willing to get to that line.

#2877. Why the Raptors could negate Jokic despite question marks at the five

The Toronto Raptors don't have a centre problem, and at the same time, they very much do.

When the Raptors take on the Denver Nuggets on Wednesday, they'll match up against the most dynamic offensive big man in the NBA – Nikola Jokic.

Despite Jokic's dominance, there's a realistic chance the Raptors produce his kryptonite. Denver currently ranks last in the league in offensive rebounds given up at 14.5 per game. Meanwhile, Toronto ranks first in offensive rebounds (15.3). Through sheer tenacity and effort, it could be enough to negate Jokic's wizardry.

It's been quite some time since Toronto's centre rotation was an undeniable advantage. When former head coach Nick Nurse had the luxury of deploying Marc Gasol and Serge Ibaka, it resulted in 48 minutes of reliable production.

Since then, the Raptors have failed to find consistency at the five.

This season, while early, has already set the stage for a fascinating narrative.

Jakob Poeltl looks healthier (and more mobile) than last season, and while he's shown positive signs of returning to form, the injury bug remains obsessed with the Austrian.

After missing the game against the Los Angeles Clippers, Poeltl was listed as questionable (left shoulder bursitis) on Nov. 3 and might be limited should he dress against Denver.

Meanwhile, Collin Murray-Boyles has taken a temporary step back, at least in comparison to what fans saw in the playoffs against the Cleveland Cavaliers. There was a lot of noise around Murray-Boyles being in even better shape this year. While the sophomore is shooting more threes, he's still ramping up defensively (and around the basket on offence).

Trayce Jackson-Davis has been a pleasant surprise off the bench, especially against the Orlando Magic on Oct. 28, when he finished with 10 points and 11 rebounds in 20 minutes of action.

In their last three games, the Raptors have both struggled and simultaneously managed their way against opposing bigs. Toronto hasn't lost the battle on the glass in any of the prior three contests, but centres have either had their way against the frontline or have made an obvious impact.

The Magic's Wendall Carter recorded 20 points and seven rebounds and Los Angeles Lakers' centre Walker Kessler finished with 16 points and 15 rebounds. While the Raptors used a historic 47-point fourth quarter to overcome the Los Angeles Clippers, the tide didn't really turn until Brook Lopez (12 points, six rebounds) fouled out.

With all due respect to the aforementioned centres, Jokic is another type of problem and represents an otherworldly challenge for the Raptors' energetic but undersized frontcourt.

Jokic is averaging 21.9 points, 11.4 rebounds and 6.6 assists through 21 games against the Raptors in his career. He also has four triple-doubles against Toronto.

#2876. Prediction markets cement their status after forecasting Democratic sweep of Congress

Prediction market platforms cemented their role in election forecasting on Tuesday, when markets accurately predicted a host of races before the polls closed, and projected outcomes before news outlets.

Democrats were seen as favored to flip both the U.S. House of Representatives and Senate on prediction markets before the first polls closed at 6:00 p.m. ET Tuesday, and were seen as narrow favorites to take at least 53 Senate seats, while a 54th was forecast as a toss-up.

So far, Democrats have won 54 Senate seats — including wins in Iowa, which prediction markets placed at 50-50 odds, and South Carolina, where Republicans narrowly favored. Outstanding results remain in Mississippi and Alaska, where prediction markets give Democrat Scott Colom and Mary Peltola 90% odds of winning.

Leading prediction market platforms were actively projecting races on X Tuesday night. Those projections, at times, came before many media outlets made their own projections, or before even a substantial number of votes had been counted.

Kalshi was confident making those calls, as long as odds for a candidate were above 99% and trading activity for the opposite candidate "dried out,"  said Jack Such, a spokesperson for the platform.

Polymarket celebrated on X how it projected races, saying that in 10 key races, the platform called races ahead of outlets including CNN, Fox News and the Associated Press by a median 31 minutes.

"It's now routinely hours between when prediction markets call it versus the TV networks," said Polymarket CEO Shayne Coplan in an X post. "No knock to them, but now there's Polymarket, and many people are realizing they want to know what's happening in real time."

Even in races where markets initially favored the candidate who eventually lost, they quickly adjusted once initial vote counts came in. In South Carolina, Democratic Senate candidate Annie Andrews was seen as the favorite on Kalshi to win shortly after 7:30 p.m., only 30 minutes after polls closed. In Texas, where Democratic state Rep. Gina Hinojosa ousted Republican Gov. Greg Abbott, Hinojosa's odds on Polymarket jumped from 39% to 73% within five minutes of initial poll closings.

"You now have financially motivated number crunchers doing this, around the world, that give you that signal just that much faster," said Flip Pidot, a member of the board of directors at the Coalition for Political Forecasting.

Still, a successful night for prediction markets shouldn't stop efforts aimed at stronger regulation of the platforms, said Michelle Kendler-Kretsch, a researcher at the Anti-Corruption Data Collective, a nonprofit. She cited a recent Reuters/Ipsos survey that found most Americans are against using prediction markets to trade on elections.

"Risks and vulnerabilities didn't change overnight just because the markets were accurate this time around," she said.

Right before the midterms, prediction market forecasts had missed major global elections, including Brazil's presidential race, where Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva only had about 20% odds to win on Kalshi before polls closed.

Prediction markets also whiffed in some high-profile Democratic primaries over the summer, forecasting extremely high odds for progressives in Wisconsin and Michigan, for example. The Michigan Democrat, Abdul El-Sayed, narrowly won his primary before going on to win Thursday's general election.

Academics who've researched prediction markets for decades say the platforms aren't wrong when candidates with low odds on markets end up winning races.

"If you toss two coins, there's a 25% chance that you'll throw two heads in a row, and nobody would be terribly surprised if you did," said Rajiv Sethi, an economics professor at Barnard College.

Despite their early success on Tuesday, Dartmouth College economic professor Eric Zitzewitz said it's still not clear if casual observers understand how market probabilities work.

"Some people who are coming to [prediction markets] for the first time will see 55 by 45 odds and think that, 'Oh the candidate with 55 is way ahead,'" he said.

As prediction markets gain traction, however, the public will improve at understanding how to interpret their odds, Zitzewitz said.

With another election under their belts, prediction markets will remain a force through the next presidential election, experts said.

"Last time around [in 2024], prediction markets were in the discussion, but they were in the discussion in a way that was a little bit unsure," said Harry Crane, a professor of statistics at Rutgers University. "This [was] the first true prediction markets election."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2875. Brent Venables on how of Florida's QB dilemma creates a challenge

The Oklahoma Sooners have a unique challenge heading into Saturday's game against the Florida Gators. The Sooners enter the game not knowing who the Gators' quarterback will be.

This isn't because of an injury; it's because of how the two have played. Aaron Philo has started most of the games this season. He came to Florida with offensive coordinator Buster Faulkner from Georgia Tech. He's completed 70.5% of his passes for 1,367 yards, nine touchdowns and five interceptions. The other quarterback is Tramell Jones Jr. He's completed 70.8% of his passes for 579 yards, four touchdowns and three interceptions this season. He started one game when Philo was unavailable, knocking off the Texas Longhorns.

Both of the quarterbacks played against the Georgia Bulldogs. Philo started and completed 5-of-7 passes for 26 yards and two interceptions. Jones replaced him and completed 18-of-25 passes for 172 yards and one interception. Head coach Brent Venables broke down the differences between the two quarterbacks and how the scheme helps them out.

"They're both very good players," Venables said. "I think, systematically, they ask them to do similar things. Philo's had more experience in the system, coming with Buster Faulkner from Georgia Tech. Both can run. Both are excellent throwers. They put the quarterback on the move and put a ton of pocket movement. They both throw the ball really, really well."

Oklahoma's preparation will have to account for both quarterbacks rather than focusing on one specific player. While Philo has more experience in Florida's system, Jones has already shown he can step into a difficult matchup and lead the Gators to a big win. Their similar skill sets also mean the Sooners cannot expect a completely different offensive approach depending on who takes the first snap.

#2874. Cardinals Brissett traded to Baltimore

Wow. How things change. And quickly, right? Four days ago, QB Jacoby Brissett was warming up on the field at AT&T Stadium in Arlington, Texas, home of the Dallas Cowboys. Today, he is a member of the Baltimore Ravens.

On Wednesday, it was announced that Cardinals GM Monti Ossenfort had traded Brissett to the Ravens. Baltimore's starting QB, Lamar Jackson, and his backup, Tyler Huntley, are both injured and landed on IR. The Ravens had undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano, and UFL quarterback Austin Reed remaining.

RELATED: CARDINALS BENCH BRISSETT IN WIN OVER COWBOYS

Brissett was pulled early in the second half of the 33-30 win over the Cowboys, and his backup, veteran Gardner Minshew, was inserted. Suddenly, the offense came alive after Dallas had dominated for two quarters. Minshew orchestrated a late drive in regulation to tie the game, then led the game-winning drive in overtime that ended with a 42-yard Chad Ryland field goal for the win.

The very next day, it was announced by Cardinals head coach Mike LaFleur that Minshew would be the starting quarterback this week against the Seattle Seahawks, and his backup would become rookie Carson Beck. This meant that Brissett had gone from starter to QB3 in less than 24 hours.

Brissett was mentioned as trade bait by several media outlets and Cardinals podcasts. That became true in a very short timespan.

The details of the trade have not been released since the deal is not complete.

It is assumed that Brissett would not dress or start at home against the Jacksonville Jaguars. The game is tomorrow night on Thursday Night Football, broadcast on Prime. The deal is announced as still pending, but will be finalized this weekend. He is a seasoned veteran with a low salary and should become a very good rental for the Ravens for the remainder of the season. Currently, Baltimore is 4-4-0 and listed last in the division.

Brissett is heading back to a division he is quite familiar with, the AFC North. He played for the Cleveland Browns in 2022 and played the first 11 games while QB Deshaun Watson was on league suspension.

#2873. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2871. Crude oil prices fall below $90 a barrel as Vance blames GOP election defeat on Iran war

Crude oil prices tumbled Wednesday after Vice President JD Vance blamed the war against Iran for Republican midterm election reversals.

U.S. West Texas Intermediate lost 8.3% to close at $88.12 a barrel. Brent crude futures, the international benchmark, shed 8.7% to settle at $94.62.

"The reason we lost is the war," Vance said in a social media post Wednesday. "The president himself said so last night."

Democrats swept the House and Senate in Tuesday's elections, giving the party leverage to pressure President Donald Trump to end the unpopular war that has spiked gasoline and diesel prices.

"The elections are leading the market to conclude that Trump might now be inclined to make a deal," said Andy Lipow, president of Lipow Oil Associates.

The Democrats may try to restrict funding for the war, Lipow said. Trump's announcement earlier this week that the U.S. will cut some funding to Israel could force Jerusalem to accept Washington's decisions, he said.

Trump told the Financial Times on Tuesday that he has no immediate plans to strike Iran. The oil market had priced in the risk of a major escalation after the elections, gaining more than 10% in October.

Oil futures have fallen more than 14% this week as senior U.S. and Iranian officials indicated they're open to a diplomatic settlement of the conflict, which has disrupted global energy supplies for eight months.

The sell-off "could be getting a bit overdone," said Rebecca Babin, senior energy trader at CIBC. The market is in a "shoot first, ask questions" later mode as emotions run high after the unexpected election results, Babin said.

Many observers expected a Democrat tsunami could push Trump to escalate with Iran, Babin said. "Vance's commentary and the public lack of backing from key allies have challenged that assumption," she said.

But the odds still favor military escalation in the region, warned Bob McNally, president of Rapidan Energy and a former advisor to President George W. Bush.

"Iran is hardened in its uncompromising position," McNally said. "Trump, having now paid a huge price for his determination to prevent Iran from obtaining a nuclear weapon, is unlikely to let go of this legacy goal and walk away."

#2869. Ravens discussion: instant reactions to the Jacoby Brissett trade

The Baltimore Ravens are acquiring Jacoby Brissett from the Arizona Cardinals, adding some much-needed quarterback depth after potential season-ending injuries to Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley. Here are Baltimore Beatdown's instant reactions to the move, and be sure to add yours in the comments section below!

I have long been a Jacoby Brissett fan. Turns out, so is Eric DeCosta! This is hardly a surprising trade considering the Ravens' injury crisis at quarterback, and Brissett is exactly the type of bridge passer I expected the team to pick up. Look, we all like Joe Fagnano, but the undrafted rookie out of UConn simply is not ready to command an NFL offense. Fans will debate if the Ravens should even be trying to win games for the rest of the season, but this franchise was never going to throw in the towel. Brissett can provide a base level of play for as long as the Ravens need it. Keeping the team's playoff hopes alive is another question entirely.

This trade seemed likely on Sunday night, but became nailed on as soon as Arizona announced Brissett would drop to QB3 on their depth chart. Although his season has been much worse than 2025 (when he had a 23/8 TD/INT ratio), he can push the ball down the field and feed the Ravens' weapons better than any of the current healthy options could. Compensation may take a while to be reported, but I suspect it won't be anything above a 5th-round pick, maybe as low as a 6th-round pick going the other way. I'm just hoping this offense is watchable. Thankfully, Brissett's debut is set to be against the Chargers. Maybe a win against Los Angeles in primetime can give the team a spark.

I'm not a fan of this. My guess is this moves means the Ravens brass is fairly confident Lamar Jackson will return this season and they want a veteran quarterback capable of keeping them within striking distance of a playoff spot. Otherwise, this truly makes no sense. In my opinion, even if Jackson is capable of coming back this year, I still think it makes no sense. This team is riddled with issues, front and center being the offensive line. While I still think it can be a good unit if they can get the desired starting five on the field, which has happened for less than a quarter of total eight games, it's still a question mark. The defense is still highly inconsistent. Their coaching staff seems to be young and learning as they go, which is fine and makes sense. But none of this adds up to a Super Bowl contending team. I am of the belief that the Ravens should be selling tradeable pieces at the deadline (Humphrey, Stanley, Andrews, Henry, Campbell, Washington, etc), locked 100% in on figuring out the Lamar Jackson extension, and building towards a massive roster overhaul to give Jesse Minter a team in his vision for 2027. Even if this trade is for as little as a 6th round pick, I don't love it. If it's for a 5th or more, I absolutely hate it and we should call DeCosta's priorities of the future into question, as well as his job.

The Ravens had to make a move to get another competent quarterback in the building and Brissett perhaps made the most sense of their options. Brissett can hopefully elevate the Ravens' offense to watchable status, but I don't expect him to move the needle very much in terms of winning games. If Lamar Jackson is able to come back at some point this season, then Brissett can hopefully keep the team afloat and in the playoff hunt, but my expectations for the rest of the season are about as low as they can get at this moment. Acquiring an established veteran like Brissett at least sends the message to players on the team that the season isn't being punted on entirely.

Jacoby Brissett provides more veteran leadership than you would have otherwise had in this QB room, and he has enough of an arm and a deep ball that he's serviceable. Not great, certainly not elite, but serviceable, which is more than what we can say about the unknowns of UDFA and practice squad crew that the Ravens had otherwise. The concern now would be what Baltimore gave up to get him (which hasn't been disclosed as of this writing) and whether they can keep him upright and healthy.

If this deal ends up being much ado about nothing as they move through a tough portion of their schedule, they can say they tried. From a roster-building standpoint, we may end up at Draft time grumbling about winning some games that didn't help them move up the board, but the front office will always prioritize trying to compete if they think things can be salvaged.

#2868. Nets vs. Hornets: Can Brooklyn finally give the home crowd something to cheer about?

Before we get into Wednesday night's matchup, I just wanted to say how great it feels to be back writing for NetsDaily. It's an honor to be part of this talented group and to have the opportunity to cover this team again.

The Brooklyn Nets are back home Wednesday night, and they'll be looking to do something they haven't done all season: win a basketball game at Barclays Center.

After finally breaking into the win column against the Indiana Pacers on Sunday, the Nets came crashing back down to earth Monday night, falling 103-90 to the New York Knicks. They've lost 15 straight games to the crosstown rivals and defending champions.

The good news? Brooklyn's next opponent isn't exactly setting the NBA on fire.

Enter the Charlotte Hornets, who have lost four straight, including an ugly 33-point loss to the Detroit Pistons on Monday. Between these two teams, somebody has to walk away feeling good about themselves Wednesday night.

Who: Charlotte Hornets (3-5) at Brooklyn Nets (1-6)

Look, nobody came into this season expecting the Nets to compete for an NBA championship. Heck, winning games was probably pretty far down the list of expectations.

But that doesn't mean we can't ask for a little entertaining basketball along the way. Brooklyn returns to Barclays Center on Wednesday night, hoping to give its home crowd its first win of the season.

They're 0-3 at home with losses against the depleted Hornets, Pacers, and Pistons. Two out of three were by double digits.

Brooklyn has struggled to find its offensive rhythm, failing to reach the century mark in three consecutive games. Monday's fourth quarter against the Knicks was particularly rough, with the Nets managing just 11 points over the final 12 minutes.

Still, there have been some bright spots. Nolan Traoré poured in 17 points against New York, while Mikel Brown Jr. chipped in 13 off the bench. For a team focused on developing its young talent, those are the kinds of performances worth keeping an eye on.

And if there's one thing that could help Brooklyn's offense Wednesday, it's Charlotte's generosity with the basketball.

The Hornets committed a whopping 27 turnovers against Detroit and have committed 82 turnovers during their four-game losing streak. That's a whole lot of extra possessions waiting to be claimed.

In the first matchup between the two teams, Charlotte dominated the inside and defeated Brooklyn 110-103, without Kon Knueppel, Grant Williams and Coby White.

On the flip side, Brooklyn forced 21 turnovers in that matchup. If the Nets can create that kind of disruption again, they'll give themselves a much better chance this time around.

Joshua Jefferson — OUT (G League Assignment)

Kon Knueppel — PROBABLE (Left Hamstring Strain)

Coby White — PROBABLE (Left Calf Injury Management)

Remember when Brandon Miller was considered a controversial pick over Scoot Henderson in the 2023 NBA Draft?

Yeah, that conversation has aged pretty well for Charlotte.

Miller is averaging 23.5 points per game to begin the season. Even though he's shooting just 37.8% from the field this season, he's capable of turning it around in the blink of an eye. Brooklyn is a team he might look to get back on track against. Charlotte won the game but Miller finished with 18 points on 7-of-21 shooting and 3-of-14 from three.

Since then, he's connected on just 22 of his last 62 field-goal attempts, so the Nets will certainly be hoping those shooting struggles make the trip to Brooklyn. Of course, knowing how these things tend to go, this would be the night he suddenly can't miss.

Either way, Wednesday presents a pretty reasonable opportunity for Brooklyn to pick up win No. 2. For those making the trip to Barclays Center, maybe even something to celebrate on the subway ride home. It's probably overdue.

#2867. Every stock that moved because of the election

The blue wave across the country has rippled through the stock market too, driving healthcare and clean energy names higher and data center plays and defense companies tumbling.

The broader market soared after Democrats won control of both the House of Representatives and the Senate, and key gubernatorial races in Texas and Georgia. In Washington, the Democratic Congress will now serve as a counterweight to President Donald Trump's agenda, which has included tax cuts and policies that sent inflation, like tariffs and the Iran war.

Local and state elections will also have an impact on data center stocks, with construction limits passed in some states and a Democratic governor-elect in Texas who promised a moratorium.

"Democratic control could slow the push for deregulation in financials, energy and digital assets," Wells Fargo Investment Institute said in a note Wednesday. "We still believe both parties have an incentive to compromise on data center location and AI generally."

Here are some of the biggest movers as a result of the election outcome.

Nextpower jumped more than 11.2 % and Canadian Solar rallied 6.7%, while Plug Power gained 3.6%, First Solar climbed 4.6% and Sunrun ticked 1.3% higher. Nextera Energy added 4.9%. Investors are hoping that Democrats will push back against the Trump administration's rejection of clean energy policies.

Healthcare companies rallied on investor optimism over a slowdown or reversal in healthcare spending cuts, and the potential that enhanced subsidies will extended in Affordable Care Act plans.

Molina Healthcare and Centene gained 8.9% and 12.6%, respectively. HCA Healthcare soared 10.3% and Universal Health Services jumped 10.9%, while Tenet Healthcare rallied 9.6% and Elevance Health added 4.9%.

Elon Musk's SpaceX and Tesla shed 3.8% and 3.3%, respectively, after Democrats won the gubernatorial, Senate, and attorney general races in Texas, as well as wresting control of the Texas House of Representatives . Musk, CEO of both companies, spent at least $474 million backing Republican candidates in the midterms, including $224 million in the Lone Star State, where both Tesla and SpaceX are headquartered.

Defense companies could face headwinds if Democrats challenge military spending. Shares of RTX slipped 2.4%, Lockheed Martin tumbled 6.9%, Northrop Grumman sank 4.5% and Howmet Aerospace fell 4.3%. Palantir Technologies shed 2.1%.

The Trump administration has requested $1.5 trillion for national defense in fiscal 2027. It is also separately asking for an $87.6 billion supplemental appropriation from Congress for fiscal 2026, largely due to the Iran conflict.

Drone stocks also fell. Kratos Defense & Security Solutions dropped 12.3%, while AeroVironment lost 8.3% and Red Cat fell 10.4%. Ondas shed 7% and Unusual Machines tumbled 9%.

Names connected with data centers moved lower amid concern about the potential for restrictions or moratoriums being placed on AI infrastructure buildouts.

Neocloud provider CoreWeave, the poster child for fears over massive AI-related debt, slumped 9.9%. Peers Nebius and Iren dropped 6.7% and 11.2%, respectively.

AI infrastructure stocks also tumbled, with Comfort Systems losing 15.1% and Vistra sliding 13.8%. Micron Technology fell 7%.

That said, five of the Magnificent Seven rallied. Apple rose 5.3% and Microsoft jumped 6.7%, with Apple touching an all-time high. Alphabet gained 3.2% and Meta added 1.4%.

A consumer relief trade also took hold, sending housing, travel and consumer staples stocks higher. Affordability was a big theme in the election, with Americans feeling the pressure of high prices.

Housing stocks like Pulte, D.R. Horton and Lennar gained roughly 7.2%, 8.1% and 7.1%, respectively. Airlines also moved higher, with United Airlines rising 8% and American Airlines climbing 6.3%. Southwest Airlines jumped 6.8% and Delta Air Lines climbed 7.2%. Cruise operator Carnival increased 7.3%, while Royal Caribbean and Norwegian Cruise Line added 6.4% and 6.8%, respectively. Booking Holdings rallied 9.8%.

Consumer staple names also caught a bid. Dollar Tree soared 9.6%, Walmart gained 3.5% and Constellation Brands climbed 5.3%. Dollar General advanced 8.5%, while Conagra Brands increased 7%.

The GOP's wide losses also extended dragged down President Donald Trump's social media and tech company, which plunged 24.5% and hit a 52-week low.

— CNBC's Luke Fountain, Dominic Chu and Nicholas Wells contributed reporting.

#2866. Heavy Duty: Wilder vs Opetaia Confirmed as Co-Main for Fury vs Joshua

The undercard for the upcoming heavyweight fight between Tyson Fury and Anthony Joshua has now added a second heavyweight showdown.

Cruiserweight champion Jai Opetaia will face former WBC World Heavyweight champion Deontay Wilder in an intriguing showdown for both men.

Fury vs Wilder and Wilder vs Opetaia will air on Netflix on Friday, December 11, from Principality Stadium in Cardiff, Wales.

If you like your boxing best when it's a pair of heavyweights slugging it out, Wednesday's news from Netflix comes as a welcome addition to the Fury vs Joshua heavyweight fight card.

Former WBC World Heavyweight champion Deontay Wilder of Tuscaloosa, Alabama (45-4-1, 43 KOs) will take on undefeated and reigning cruiserweight champion Jai Opetaia of Australia (31-0, 24 KOs), who will give the heavyweight division a go for the first time as a professional.

It's an unexpected but not an unwelcome matchup. Opetaia appeared to be on a collision course for a cruiserweight division unification fight with David Benavidez, currently the unified WBA and WBO champion. Benavidez was waved into the ring by Opetaia after he defeated Noel Mikaelian in October, and the pair seemed eager for a showdown.

Instead, Benavidez is now scheduled to face his mandatory challenger and the WBC World Cruiserweight champion, Michał Cieślak of Poland, on December 5 in Glendale, Arizona.

The only remaining title in play is the IBF belt. It originally belonged to Opetaia, but the sanctioning organization stripped Opetaia of the title on March 19, 2026, after he participated in an unsanctioned March 8 fight against Brandon Glanton under Zuffa Boxing, which the IBF does not recognize.

But who needs a belt at stake when there's a big Netflix payday on the line?

Deontay Wilder may be a step slower but he is still a big puncher. Photo: Photo: MF Boxing / Wasserman

Although he is well past his prime at age 41, Wilder remains among the most feared punchers in heavyweight history. He will bring a 95.65% knockout rate and a significant height and reach advantage into the fight against Opetaia.

A decade younger at age 31, Opetaia is widely regarded as a solid puncher himself and was the reigning force in the cruiserweight division until David Benavidez Jr. came along. He won the IBF cruiserweight world title in 2022. Now, he moves up to heavyweight for the biggest test of his career against Wilder.

Like Wilder, he is also an Olympian, competing for Australia in 2012 at age 16. Wilder was part of the 2008 U.S. Olympic Team and brought home a bronze medal from Beijing.

Now, he moves up to heavyweight for the biggest test of his career against Wilder, who held the WBC heavyweight world title from 2015 to 2020 and dominated as one of the hardest punchers in boxing history. He is best known for his fierce trilogy of fights against Tyson Fury between 2018 and 2021.

Wilder is 6-foot-7 to Opetaia's 6-foot-1. Wilder has an 83-inch reach; Opetaia has a 76-inch reach. Opetaia is the better boxer, but Wilder is without a doubt still the harder puncher even in his 40s.

Wilder earned a split decision in an entertaining if messy fight against Derek Chisora in April. Opetaia scored a ninth-round TKO win over Noel Mikaelian in September on the Garcia vs Benn undercard in Las Vegas.

Anthony Joshua and Eddie Hearn were all smiles at the Fury vs Wilder kickoff press conference. No Dana White in sight. Photo: Zacharias Delgado, Matchroom Boxing

Once Netflix made the Opetaia vs Wilder fight official Wednesday, boxing observers put two and two together. Just as the "Promotergate" drama between Dana White and Eddie Hearn has subsided, it threatens to come back to life.

White is Opetaia's promoter through Zuffa Boxing. While Hearn says the contract with Joshua for the main event with Fury guaranteed he would be the promoter, he can hardly deny White access now that one of his fighters is involved.

White wasted no time posting gleeful announcements about the fight on his social media channels.

If Misfits Boxing promoter Kalle Sauerland is to be believed, he claims to have an offer on the table for a winner-take-all boxing match between the two promoters to settle their differences, with a $10 million purse at stake.

In an interview Wednesday with IFL TV in England, Sauerland claims he is "not far off" with Eddie, but he has not been in touch with White, although he says White has the offer in writing. Sauerland says the offer is genuine.

But he has also joked about staging the fight the day after the Fury vs Joshua card at Tottenham Hotspur Stadium. Don't make any travel plans just yet.

Since signing to Zuffa Boxing, Opetaia has been stripped of his IBF title, although he still retains the Ring Magazine belt as the lineal champion. He is, of course, the Zuffa Boxing cruiserweight champion. But Zuffa Boxing signed Opetaia in part to lure other fighters to the fold, particularly David Benavidez Jr.

If we aren't going to see David Benavidez vs Jai Opetaia anytime soon, Wilder vs Opetaia will do. Photo: Al Powers/Zuffa Boxing via Getty Images

Now that Benavidez Jr. is making a serious run at becoming the undisputed champion at cruiserweight and possibly taking on the previous undisputed cruiserweight champion in Oleksandr Usyk, Opetaia is left standing against the wall at the dance without a partner.

So why not try his hand at heavyweight? Belts matter a bit less in the heavyweight division. Heavyweights are a big draw, earning big money whether or not they have titles.

Wilder presents a recognizable name, and given his activity and age advantage, Opetaia and his team see it as a risk worth taking.

But Zuffa is serving up one of their biggest names to an opponent who is a serious threat. Can Zuffa Boxing and Dana White afford to have Opetaia lose to Wilder?

Wilder has nothing to lose. He's near the end of his career. Earning a big payday lines him up improbably and nicely for a second big payday.

Imagine if Anthony Joshua defeats Tyson Fury, and Wilder beats Opetaia. Will we be rooting for this outcome to see Joshua versus Wilder in 2027? I wouldn't hate you if you did.

Wilder also gives American boxing fans another reason to watch on December 11, aside fro the two British fighters in the main event and the Australian opponent in the co-main that many fans still don't know.

One thing is sure: the formal announcement press conference with all players present will be must-see-TV.  Stick with NY Fights for the latest coverage of Hearn vs White and Fury vs Joshua.

#2865. Giants' Abdul Carter, Cam Skattebo remain day-to-day ahead of Week 9 matchup with Eagles

The Giants' battle with a shorthanded roster continues.

After practice Wednesday, coach John Harbaugh said outside linebacker Abdul Carter and running back Cam Skattebo remain day-to-day as the Big Blue approach their Week 9 contest against their NFC East rival, the Philadelphia Eagles.

Though he wasn't seen at practice, Carter is improving, Harbaugh said.

"Abdul should be fine," Harbaugh said. "He's sick, he [has an] illness, so he should be fine," Harbaugh said.

He wasn't so sure about Skattebo, who tweaked his hamstring in Week 7 and sat on the sidelines with a trainer during practice.

"Skatt's got a hamstring [injury], so you know how that goes," he added. "We'll see."

With Skattebo's availability uncertain for Sunday and Tyron Tracy out on injured reserve, the Giants' are left with Devin Singletary as their most experienced halfback. Then there's rookie third-string Greg Desrosiers Jr., who New York signed to its 53-man roster Oct. 27 after Tracy's injury was confirmed, and Eric Gray, who missed the first seven games of the season with a knee injury.

But Harbaugh said he's not worried about his team's ability to adapt and produce on Sunday regardless.

"I feel very confident about the running back situation. I like the guys we've got," Harbaugh said. "Obviously, [Devin's] been here. We've got some guys that have been here in the past, too, that we brought in ... — Eric — so, I feel good about it. We claimed [Desrosiers] off the Chargers. He's a very talented, young guy. So, those guys are in practice. They've got two practices now, and if they have to go, they'll definitely be ready to roll."

However, Skattebo and Carter were the only two Giants not practicing Wednesday. Right tackle Jermaine Eluemunor, linebacker Malik Harrison, cornerback Colton Hood, wide receiver Charlie Jones, and left tackle Andrew Thoms were limited, while tight end Theo Johnson, offensive lineman Marcus Mbow, and defensive lineman DJ Reader participated in full.

#2864. Democrats take Congress in a blue wave. Here's what it means for your money

With Democrats flipping control of Congress in the midterm election, the shift in power on Capitol Hill could reshape household budgets — from student loan bills and health insurance premiums to housing costs and taxes.

Experts say results show that rising costs were top of mind for voters.

"I see this election as a repudiation of the anti-affordability agenda from the Trump administration," said Daniel Bunn, president and CEO of the Tax Foundation, a nonpartisan, nonprofit research organization.

"Whether that's the conflict in the Middle East or the tariffs, just top to bottom, it's clear to me that was a real concern of the electorate," he said.

White House spokesman Matthew Middleton said in an emailed statement that the Trump administration has delivered "tangible wins to make life more affordable for American families." Officials "will continue to put hardworking American's first with or without support from Democrats," he wrote.

Democrats won control of both the House of Representatives and Senate, which could make it easier for the party to advance legislation. They can also use hearings and investigations to press federal agencies and companies to answer for policies and practices that impact consumers, and exert leverage over confirmation of President Donald Trump's nominees.

But turning campaign promises into changes people feel in their finances won't be easy. Trump can veto bills a Democrat-controlled Congress passes, limiting how much of their agenda could become law. Based on projections, Democrats will lack the numbers needed to override a presidential veto.

Any changes will also take time: Newly elected members of Congress won't take office until January.

Here are eight ways Democrats’ new congressional majority could affect your money.

"Not surprisingly, the Dems are going to be keyed in on affordability with respect to healthcare," said Zach Gaumer, vice president of health policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonpartisan think tank.

OBBBA made Trump's 2017 tax cuts permanent and added temporary tax breaks. These included new deductions for tip income, overtime earnings, seniors and auto loan interest, which will expire after 2028 without an extension.

Democrats may support extending these provisions. But as the Democrat-controlled Congress maps out its agenda, lawmakers will have to address the federal budget deficit. For fiscal year 2026, the budget deficit ballooned to nearly $2 trillion, the Treasury announced in October.

Newly elected Democratic senators James Talarico of Texas, Troy Jackson of Maine and Annie Andrews of South Carolina all campaigned on expanding the child tax credit. Earlier this year, Senate Democrats also floated ideas to expand the standard deduction and create income tax exemptions for lower-earning Americans — which are costly proposals, according to Bunn from the Tax Foundation.

"I've called these termites in the tax code," he said. "You cannot consistently narrow your base, especially for the income tax, and expect your spending priorities to be well-funded."

With Democrats in control of Congress, they are likely to hold hearings and subpoena Trump officials over the administration's student loan policies, said higher education expert Mark Kantrowitz.

One priority may be pressing the Education Department to fix the popular Public Service Loan Forgiveness program, which cancels the remaining federal student debt of eligible government and nonprofit workers after a decade. Over the summer, Trump officials reassessed repayment timelines, delaying some borrowers' forgiveness date by years.

Broad student loan forgiveness is unlikely: Trump could veto such a bill, and the Supreme Court blocked the Biden administration's plan. More attainable changes could include restoring tax-free treatment of forgiven student debt, raising the new borrowing caps and expanding repayment options — measures that may attract bipartisan support, Kantrowitz said.

Trump has also said he may support legislation capping federal student loan interest rates, a proposal that could give Democrats common ground in their push to ease borrowers’ costs.

Housing affordability was a key election issue, and Democrats will likely explore ways to deliver relief, such as introducing measures to help first-time homebuyers, according to an Oct. 30 analysis by Desjardins, a Canadian financial services company.

However, lawmakers generally have little direct impact on home prices, which are based on market conditions. Mortgage rates, too, are influenced by forces generally beyond congressional control. The yield on the 10-year Treasury, which remains higher than it was before the Iran war — due at least partly to concerns about inflation eroding the value of those bonds — influences mortgage rates.

"Jumpstarting the housing market relies on a calmer geopolitical environment and bond market in the short run," plus more housing supply and a "more dynamic labor market in the long run," said Jake Krimmel, senior economist at Realtor.com.

The bipartisan 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act, which automatically became law July 11 without the president's signature due to a constitutional provision, is "a model for what can pass in a divided Washington," Krimmel said. It includes measures to encourage housing construction, reduce regulatory barriers, expand financing options and limit purchases of single-family homes by large institutional investors.

The incoming class of senators will have to address Social Security insolvency.

The program's trustees project that the trust fund Social Security relies on to help pay retirement benefits, formally known as the Old-Age and Survivors Insurance Trust Fund, may be depleted in the fourth quarter of 2032. Without action from Congress before then, benefits could face an across-the-board cut of 22%.

A number of Democratic lawmakers and some Republicans have called for increased payroll taxes on high earners to help shore up Social Security.

A potential SECURE 3.0 bill — a follow-up to bipartisan retirement-savings laws passed in 2019 and 2022 — could help expand workers' access to workplace retirement plans like 401(k)s, for example, among a roster of other reforms, experts said.

"Trump promised to cap credit card rates at 10%," said Aaron Klein, a senior fellow in economic studies at the Brookings Institution, a think tank. "A Democratic Congress might be willing to do that."

Any such cap is likely to be closer to 36% than to the 10% cap Trump has sought, Jaret Seiberg, a financial services policy analyst at TD Cowen, an investment bank, wrote in a research note Wednesday. Lawmakers in the current Congress have proposed legislation with these thresholds.

A cap would likely be welcome news for low- and middle-income Americans, Klein said. They are more likely than wealthy households to use debt to finance their lives and to carry a credit card balance — and therefore owe interest on their charges, he said.

Cardholders might see other impacts of such a policy, such as lower credit limits and a clawback of certain card rewards, he said.

Democrats' takeover of Congress could stall efforts to put crypto-friendly regulations into federal law, but everyday investors shouldn't expect much to change immediately.

"Platforms will keep offering the products they offer today, and they still have friendly regulators at the SEC and CFTC who are approving an ever-wider range of crypto products," said Lee Reiners, a cryptocurrency regulation expert and lecturing fellow of economics at Duke University.

The election results could threaten the Clarity Act, which would give the Commodity Futures Trading Commission greater authority over cryptocurrency trading while reducing the Securities and Exchange Commission's role. The bill passed the House in July 2025 by a 294-134 vote, but failed to advance in the Senate in September on a 49-50 procedural vote. "CLARITY is dead," said Reiners, adding that he sees little chance of the bill passing during the lame-duck session before Democrats take control in January.

Any future legislation aimed at creating a regulatory framework for digital assets would likely need to address Democrats' concerns about investor protections and conflicts of interest involving Trump's crypto businesses, said Martin Mulyadi, an accounting professor at Shenandoah University.

The SEC and CFTC can also continue developing crypto-friendly rules without Congress. But those rules are easier for a future administration to reverse than legislation, said Marcel Miu, a certified financial planner and founder of Simplify Wealth Planning based in Austin, Texas. "I'd tell investors to size their crypto investments as if the rules could change repeatedly over the coming years," Miu said.

The Iran war has led oil prices — and those of gasoline, jet fuel, heating oil, diesel and other fuels refined from oil — to surge. That has trickled into higher prices across the U.S. economy, experts said.

Oil prices fell on Wednesday on hopes that the election outcome will push Trump to make a deal to end the war.

"Ending the war in Iran would be the biggest policy lever to reducing inflation," the Brookings Institution's Klein said.

A Democrat-led Congress could try to limit Trump's ability to continue the war, perhaps by cutting funding.

"Congress' biggest power is the power of the purse," Klein said. "Wars are expensive."

— CNBC's Lorie Konish and Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting.

#2863. Cavaliers reach two-way agreement with familiar face

It was reported by Michael Scotto of Hoop Hype that the Cleveland Cavaliers will be signing 6'5", 25-year-old forward Norchad Omier to a two-way contract.

It was originally reported that Omier had agreed to a two-way deal with the Minnesota Timberwolves, however, Scotto reported that Omier has "changed his mind" and instead "plans to sign a two-way deal with the Cleveland Cavaliers."

This is certainly an unusual situation for obtaining a two-way deal, however, this presents the Cavaliers with another option for backup depth. Omier started last season with the Charge, before signing a two-way contract with the Los Angeles Clippers. Omier appeared in 38 G League games last season, averaging 18.3 points and 11.4 rebounds on .592/.291/.787 shooting splits.

Omier spent training camp with the Timberwolves. He played six preseason games with them this season, and averaged 1.8 points and 3.2 rebounds per preseason appearance.

It hasn't been confirmed as of yet which of the Cavaliers current two-way players (Tristan Enaruna, Riley Minix or Ernest Udeh Jr) will be waived in order to complete this move. Two-way contracts are not guaranteed, which means the team can cut a player at any time without penalty.

Omier has some history with the Charge, in 2025-2026, Omier posted a 20 points, 11.9 rebounds, and 2.7 assists per game split over 20 regular-season contests. Obviously, both sides had interest in a reunion, and the question remained of which of the Cavaliers' two-way options will be let go to make room for this transaction.

#2862. Lions, Dolphins Week 9 injury report: Brian Branch limited Wednesday

The Detroit Lions (5-2) are back on the road in Week 9, traveling to Florida to take on the Miami Dolphins (1-6). The Lions held a walkthrough on Wednesday instead of a full practice, so all participation levels are estimates from the coaching staff based on how the player would have participated in a traditional practice.

With that in mind, let's look at Wednesday's injury report and get you up to date on what we know.

Note: Any changes from the most recent injury report will be bolded.

RB Isiah Pacheco (MCL/Back, IR) — expected to be out until December

OL Giovanni Manu (undisclosed, NFI) — eligible to begin practicing at any time

Rolder injured his hamstring last Sunday, and with the injury potentially requiring surgery, he's being shut down for the remainder of the season. While that ends his rookie season, the good news is he should be able to return in 2027.

I wanted to take a moment to talk about Jack Campbell, as most Lions fans are anxiously awaiting updates on his status and wondering about his future, both on and off the field. First, in case you missed it, Jack Campbell released a statement earlier on Wednesday; here's an excerpt (emphasis is the author's):

"I'm incredibly encouraged by the progress I've made in such a short period of time. I have full movement in my lower body, and I'm continuing to make progress with movement in my upper body. I know there's still work ahead, but I remain very optimistic. I'm taking things one day at a time, staying positive, and focusing on getting better."

Jack is still on the 53-man roster and hasn't been moved to injured reserve, but it would be improper to speculate why a move has or hasn't been made at this time. The team typically takes their time in these situations, so it's important to be patient right now and not make assumptions either way.

C Cade Mays (ankle) — Injured in Week 8 — Preparing for Juice Scruggs to start

EDGE DJ Wonnum (concussion) — Injured in Week 8

LB Jack Campbell (neck) — Injured in Week 8

Mays reportedly has a "low" ankle injury, which typically sidelines players from one to six weeks (high ankle injuries usually last four to 12 weeks), and while we don't know how long this will sideline the Lions' starting center, Dan Campbell did note that he missing Wednesday's practice was planned and Thursday's practice could also be in jeopardy. For now, the team is preparing to turn to Juice Scruggs against the Dolphins.

Wonnum is in the NFL's concussion protocol and must pass a series of steps before he can move forward. Every player deals with head injuries differently, so we will have to wait and see how he is able to handle the increasing stressors deployed in the protocol.

Reed was forced out of the Vikings game late, and while he didn't return, he was available in the locker room after the game, which is typically a good sign for his injury. The Lions will likely take it slow with their top corner this week.

RB Jacob Saylors (neck) — Injured in Week 8

WR Amon-Ra St. Brown (rib) — Injured in Week 8

LB Amen Ogbongbemiga (toe) — Injured in Week 8

S Kerby Joseph (knee, PUP) — Days remaining in injury evaluation clock: 13

S Brian Branch (concussion) — Injured in Week 8

Saylors was limited early in practices in Week 8 with a neck injury, and while he did not carry an injury designation into the game against the Vikings, he did exit with a reported neck injury. It's unclear whether this is a new injury or a tweak of the existing one, but it's not surprising to see him get early-week rest to allow more time to recover.

Last week, St. Brown was dealing with an ankle injury, and now he's dealing with a rib injury. He left Sunday's game against the Vikings briefly but did not miss much time before returning.

Ogbongbemiga is dealing with a toe injury, which could leave Detroit's linebacker room a little short this week, but given he's just listed as limited, there is plenty of time for him to recover. He could play a bigger special teams role this week, with Malcolm Rodriguez and Trevor Nowaske expected to play bigger defensive roles.

On Tuesday, coach Dan Campbell discussed the timeline for Kerby Joseph, noting that he won't play in Miami this weekend, as he's still slowly acclimating back to football activities.

"We're going to try and give him some scout team reps. He's not going to play this game," Campbell said. "I know that (question) was coming. He's not playing vs. Miami. He's just going to get some reps in practice."

Branch, like Wonnum, must go through the NFL's structured concussion protocol, but an estimated limited practice is a positive sign he could play this week.

#2861. Raiders activate TE from IR, release wideout 3rd on team in TD's

Wednesday the Raiders were at the deadline for activating tight end Carter Runyon from injured reserve or shut him down for the season. They opted to activate the promising second-year tight end, which meant they had to make a corresponding move to make room.

The corresponding move turned out to be WR Cody White.

White was signed to the team after being let go by the Seahawks in their final roster cuts at the end of August. He was elevated to the roster the first three weeks of the season, and caught three touchdowns in those games. A mark that is still third on the team and second among wide receivers on the roster below only Tre Tucker (four)

With three elevations, that meant if they wanted to use him again, they would need to sign him. They did so, while sending WR Jack Bech to injured reserve.

Well, now Jack Bech is back. As is Dont'e Thornton. And Jalen Nailor had his best game last Sunday, catching his first touchdown. So, apparently when the decision had to be made as to who was expendable, it was White.

I would expect the team to add him back to the practice squad should he clear waivers. Keep in mind when they activated Thornton, they waived Jonah Laulu and he was claimed by the Titans, so they were unable to get him back.

#2860. Sixers face Pistons with chance to avenge lone loss of the season

The Philadelphia 76ers begin a three-game road trip on Wednesday with a visit to the Detroit Pistons.

It'll be a battle of two teams enjoying red-hot starts to their 2026-27 seasons, with the Sixers coming into the matchup 7-1 and the Pistons 6-1. It's worth noting that the Sixers' lone loss so far this campaign came at the hands of the Pistons back on Oct. 25.

The Sixers' injury report is as clean as can be expected for this one, with only Dominick Barlow (back) and Ariel Hukporti (Achilles) on the list. For Detroit, they are still without Jalen Duren, who suffered a right hamstring strain in the second game of the season and has not returned since. They will also be without former 76er Isaiah Joe with a right hip contusion.

The loss of Duren as it happened seemed like it could be something that set the Pistons back a bit, but they've managed just fine so far. Cade Cunningham continues to be an effective offensive engine for Detroit, posting 23.7 points and 7.1 assists per game this season and is shooting 37.0% from deep on a 6.6 attempts-per-game clip.

In Duren's absence, former Sixer Paul Reed has stepped up in taking on the starting five position. Reed has started the last five contests for the Pistons and has averaged 13.8 points, 6.0 rebounds, 2.0 assists. 1.6 steals and 1.4 blocks in that stint. Not only that, but Reed is shooting 65.0% from the floor (5.6-for-8.6) to start the year. The Sixers would love a backup center like that…

Looking at team numbers, the Pistons are currently the best in the association in defensive rating at 102.0, but the Sixers are right there with them in third at 105.1. Detroit is holding opposing offenses to shooting just 41.2% from the floor, the lowest mark in the league. The best way to break through that wall for Philly may be to do exactly what they did against the Bulls (who, yes, are a much worse team than the Pistons) in getting out ahead in transition as often and as quickly as possible. Giving them time to set up defensively will only make things harder for the Sixers, who are still admittedly finding their footing at times in the half court offense — something one would hope only gets better and better with more time together on the floor.

As I mentioned, the Sixers' only loss this season so far came at the hands of this Detroit squad when they faced off in South Philadelphia over a week ago, but the context surrounding that game is worth noting as well. The Sixers went into that matchup on the second leg of a back-to-back (versus the Pistons after a day off), with both Joel Embiid and VJ Edgecombe sidelined. Don't get me wrong, I'm not saying that because they've had some rest and are at full strength that it'll be some cakewalk for Philadelphia tonight… but I think it certainly won't hurt their cause.

Both teams come into Wednesday on a three-game winning streak, but only one can remain near-perfect on the season after tonight.

The Sixers and Pistons tip off at 7 p.m. ET.

#2859. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."
