#2858. Democrats map out an agenda to address affordability and corruption after midterm sweep

The newly elected Democratic majorities in the Senate and the House are planning to pass a flurry of bills as soon as they take the reins next January, CNBC has learned, and are preparing to tussle with an antagonistic President Donald Trump.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The legislative push aims to show a united Democratic caucus taking quick action on the issues they were elected to solve, especially oppressively high costs on everything from gas to groceries.

In their first 100 hours after being sworn in, House Democrats plan to bring to the floor bills reinstating the enhanced health insurance subsidies that expired under the GOP-held Congress, and reversing the Medicaid cuts Trump signed into law last year.

Democrats — likely led by Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries (D-N.Y.), who is poised to become the first Black Speaker of the House in U.S. history — also plan to push forward a bill that would undo Trump's sweeping tariffs and reassert Congress' role in setting import taxes. And they will again vote on legislation to end the Iran war or force Trump to seek congressional authorization to keep it going. Some details of the Democrats' plans had been previously reported by the New York Times.

But Democrats will need to strike a balance between these legislative priorities and a robust oversight and investigatory agenda that the party is mapping out to scrutinize Trump, his family, cabinet and supporters in the business world.

"We haven't evaluated who are going to be the targets of the investigation right out of the gate. We'll have those conversations over the next couple of weeks," Jeffries told CNBC on Wednesday.

"But clearly, things aren't going right for the American people. Capitalism is broken in the way in which it is being experienced by so many working-class Americans and middle-class Americans. We've got to make sure we build an economy, a free market economy, that works for everyday Americans," Jeffries said.

Democrats on the campaign trail for months hammered home a message of affordability. And as corruption emerged as a top issue for voters in the waning months of the election, they tried to connect the Trump administration's perceived bad behavior to Americans' pocketbooks.

Already, prominent Democrats are taking a similar tack as they prepare to assume majorities in both chambers.

Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon, the top Democrat on the Senate Finance Committee, said in a statement on Wednesday that the panel's top priorities "will be affordability for us, accountability for them."

Wyden said the committee would double down on its efforts to make sure billionaires pay their fair share in taxes, address the "corporate chokehold on our nation's health care system" and reassert congressional authority over trade. He also vowed to target the "special interests" that had cozied up to Trump in the past two years.

"From the banks that helped cover up Epstein's crimes, to insurance companies that are delaying and denying health care to churn a profit, to the pharmaceutical company CEOs that price gouge while enjoying Trump's hand-delivered windfalls, when I am chair, they will be forced to answer to the Senate Finance Committee for their role in hurting the American people and enabling Trump's corruption," Wyden said.

Rep. Robert Garcia of California, the top-ranking Democrat on the House Oversight Committee, has vowed to take on Trump's family, corporate corruption, Department of Homeland Security and Immigration and Customs Enforcement overreach and the handling of files related to disgraced sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

Garcia issued a pointed, two-word statement as results tipped toward Democrats on Tuesday night: "Lawyer up."

And Rep. Pramila Jayapal, D-Wash., a former chair of the Congressional Progressive Caucus and member of the House Judiciary and Foreign Affairs committees, said in a recent interview with CNBC that she believed Democrats should target figures like Elon Musk, as well as cabinet secretaries like Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, Secretary of Health and Human Services Robert F. Kennedy Jr., and Attorney General Todd Blanche.

But she acknowledged the challenges that could come with pursuing a large number of potential targets all at once, especially with a potentially unwieldy and ideologically diverse Democratic caucus.

"I think what we should do is pick one or two cases that really exemplify the worst of the corruption, and that we feel we have the most evidence of wrongdoing," Jayapal said. "There are so many things — I think it's important to focus on a couple."

Meanwhile, other Democrats are angling for some kind of ethics reform package that could include prohibitions on stock trading and use of prediction markets for government officials, a federal ban on gerrymandering and limiting super PAC spending.

"My expectation is that when Democrats are the majority, we will pass a strong bill to ban stock trading in Congress, in the executive branch, and beyond, and that this will be part of a larger package of ethics reforms that we pursue," said Rep. Seth Magaziner, D-R.I., a longtime advocate for such legislation.

#2856. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2854. ‘She Belongs in the Conversation’ – ESPN Icon Linda Cohn Floats 5-Time WNBA All-Star As Chris DeMarco’s Replacement for Liberty’s HC Job

The New York Liberty are searching for a new head coach after firing Chris DeMarco following one season, and ESPN legend Linda Cohn has a familiar name in mind as the franchise weighs its next move.

The candidate has deep ties to New York along with coaching experience in the WNBA, NBA, and college basketball.

Cohn praised Teresa Weatherspoon while discussing the Liberty, saying, "Teresa Weatherspoon. You want a band that would get Liberty some big-time fan attention. Spoon. A franchise icon with the Liberty. With professional coaching experience, including as a WNBA head coach. Why not T-Spoon? She belongs in the conversation when you're talking about the next head coach for the Liberty."

The Hall of Famer is one of the WNBA's pioneers and remains a popular figure in New York. She played seven seasons with the Liberty, earning five All-Star selections and winning WNBA Defensive Player of the Year twice.

The former Liberty star also helped guide New York to the WNBA Finals four times and made one of the most iconic shots in league history during the 1999 Finals. She hit a half-court game-winner in Game 2 against the Houston Comets.

Her coaching career includes six seasons leading Louisiana Tech, where she compiled a 99-71 record and guided the Lady Techsters to consecutive NCAA Tournament appearances in 2010 and 2011. She also won the Western Athletic Conference Tournament championship in 2010.

The five-time All-Star previously worked in player development for the Liberty before joining the New Orleans Pelicans in 2019. She initially worked with the team's two-way players and later served as an NBA assistant coach.

She returned to the WNBA as head coach of the Chicago Sky in 2024. Chicago finished 13-27 and missed the playoffs, and she was fired after one season. The decision drew criticism from Angel Reese, who had played her rookie season under Weatherspoon.

DeMarco, meanwhile, guided the Liberty through injuries to key players, including Sabrina Ionescu and Satou Sabally, and helped the team win the Commissioner's Cup. New York also upset the Minnesota Lynx in the first round of the playoffs before losing to the Atlanta Dream in the semifinals.

RELATED: 'A Big, Big Mistake' – ESPN Legend Slams Liberty for Firing Sandy Brondello As Her Replacement Chris DeMarco Departs After 1 Season

DeMarco faced criticism after New York failed to use a reset timeout late in Game 2 against Atlanta. Questions about his future grew after Breanna Stewart, Ionescu and Jonquel Jones declined to endorse him following the season. The Liberty subsequently fired him after one year.

Weatherspoon's history with New York extends beyond her playing career. She also worked in franchise development during her previous stint with the Liberty, giving her experience both within the organization and on the sidelines.

If she started a second chapter as a WNBA head coach, Cohn believes there's no better place than where she made history.

#2853. What was really behind Wednesday's monster rally? Trump quickly making peace

The stock market skyrocketed on Wednesday on the heels of a decisive sweep for Democrats in the 2026 midterm elections. But that was not necessarily the only driver of the climb.

"President Trump's acknowledgement of the Democrats' victory in the midterms and his expressed openness to collaborating with the opposition have helped markets to rally on hopes that contestation of the results and more adverse polarization can be avoided," said Thomas Hempell, Generali Investments senior economist.

Trump told The New York Times and The Wall Street Journal overnight Tuesday that he accepted the results and would work with Democrats. Those headlines coincided with a rebound in stock market futures.

In the lead-up to this year's midterms, some on Wall Street grew increasingly concerned about the prospect of Trump contesting the election results. The Department of Justice's Alaska election lawsuit led to fears over delays with the outcome that would exacerbate the uneasiness in the market that's typically removed once the elections conclude.

CNBC's Fed Survey showed that among 30 fund managers, strategists and economists, 63% believe that the extended uncertainty around the balance of power created by legal challenges to the election results posed "some" market risk. That was a slight increase from 61% in September. Additionally, 14% believed it represented "significant" market risk, which was up from 7%.

Now, investors are breathing a sigh of relief over Trump's acceptance of the results, which should "act as a break on extreme policy outcomes and bring down risk premia," so long as the president's decision remains, said George Saravelos, head of FX Research at Deutsche Bank.

Trump has a history of rallying markets, as well as driving them lower, with his announcements. Jay Woods of Freedom Capital Markets, who noted that the rebound in stocks overnight was partially due to the president's "initial tone and reaction to the results," said the focus will shift to whether Trump's latest move will actually have staying power.

"I think Trump is going to try to do and say the right thing, but at two in the morning, he may change his mind," the chief market strategist said. "He is an X factor."

Woods added that he's "encouraged" by Wednesday's massive rally, but he noted that there are still headwinds that could plague the market over the coming weeks — such as concerns about inflationary pressures as a result of higher energy prices from the conflict in the Middle East.

"How Trump reacts to it will help us navigate those headwinds," he said.

Osaic's Philip Blancato said he believes that if Trump "continues to play nice" with Democrats, the market is going to have a "very strong" end to the year.

Stocks historically tend to rally after the midterms anyway, but this time around, some were worried the solid run-up in the S&P 500 this year was going to potentially hinder those gains. After Trump's reaction, Blancato said he doesn't think the S&P 500 hitting 8,000 by year-end is totally out of the question and that there's "a lot predicated on him, but not completely."

The broad market index gained more than 1% on Wednesday, with its closing price implying a roughly 4% gain to that level.

However, investors should be prepared for any future volatility if Trump's rhetoric does not remain the same, the chief market strategist said.

"The president's always going to be a wildcard," he said. "Let's not let one day make a year. This is an instant reaction to this thing. There's still plenty of things to be hashed out."

#2852. Fantasy Football Rankings: Hayden Winks' RB Blueprint for Week 9

The Blueprint is my weekly column and basically everything that I use to build out the most accurate rankings I can. There will be this RB column, a WR column and a combination column on the onesie positions. You will find film notes and endless charts. Maybe enough charts to stress test the Yahoo website. I kid.

It's impossible to get every ranking thoroughly early in the week, so be sure to keep checking in on these columns through Sunday morning. As always, my full rankings at every position are available.

1. Jahmyr Gibbs (at MIA) - 133.5 proj. total yards

Gibbs survived the Vikings matchup with a top-5 finish in Week 8. Things will get easier from there. A lot easier. Across the season, Gibbs is averaging 25.5 half PPR points (RB1) on 23.7 expected points (RB1). He's leading in carries per game and second in receptions per game. What a season.

Miami ranks ninth-worst in half PPR points allowed to opposing backfields (22.5 per game) and sits second in receiving points allowed to RBs. Detroit carries an implied team total of 28.75 points (third) as 7-point road favorites. This is obviously a smash spot, duh.

2. Kenneth Walker III (vs. NYJ) - 113.5 proj. total yards

Walker has had slower games against the Seahawks and healthy Broncos defenses, both clearly top-5 units to me right now. It hasn't helped that LT Josh Simmons (back/other) and LG Kingsley Suamataia (benched) haven't been active, forcing the Chiefs to dial up a ton of their runs to the right side only. Still, Walker is the RB6 on RB3 usage this month and RB2 in both metrics across the entire season. The offense at least getting Rashee Rice back in the mix will help them move the ball more often.

The Jets placed star DT T'Vondre Sweat on IR and first-round rookie DE David Bailey has missed time recently, too. They bottled up Mike Washington Jr. and the Raiders' disaster-class OL last week, but they're a unit to attack right now. Kansas City carries an implied total of 28 points (fourth) as 9.5-point home favorites.

3. Christian McCaffrey (vs. LV) - 96.5 proj. total yards

McCaffrey is the RB6 in half PPR points per game on RB6 usage across his last four games. They needed more receiving work from CMC during this stretch with Deebo Samuel Sr. and Mike Evans missing time, and we'll see if they return off the bye. He's better without them but has been a mid-range RB1 with the WRs healthy.

The Raiders rank fifth in half PPR points allowed to opposing backfields (23.4 per game) and sit third in rushing points allowed to RBs, recently surrendering an RB6 finish to Braelon Allen. San Francisco enters with a slate-high 30.5-point implied team total (first) as 9.5-point home favorites. It's a great spot.

4. Bijan Robinson (vs. CIN) - 122.5 proj. total yards

Robinson is the RB30 on RB23 usage this month. I can promise that'll be the worst stretch of his season.

The primary driver of the falloff is the on/off splits with Michael Penix Jr. as a receiver. His targets with Penix this year are 3.2 per game. His career average for context is 5.1. Part of this is Penix's inability to move, which is required to buy time in the screen game or to work to the checkdown. The ball is flying out of his hand early in the snap, and those are less likely to go to RBs. Hopefully they carve out more looks moving forward. It'd be enough of a difference to view him in the mid-range RB1 tier, instead of the elite RB1 tier.

The Bengals are much improved against the run with superstar DT Dexter Lawrence shutting down everything up the middle, but the rest of the defense is looking suspect, especially if CB1 DJ Turner (ankle) can't go. The LB and S groups are missing huge tackles weekly, and Robinson is a great candidate to rip wide zone explosives away from Lawrence in the A gap. This is what Tony Pollard did last week when he was the RB3 overall. The Falcons carry a 24.25-point implied team total (12th) as 3-point home underdogs.

5. Javonte Williams (at IND) - 83.5 proj. total yards

Williams' lack of explosives is starting to cause the Cowboys' games, like last week in the second half when they leaned on the run game and it went nowhere. For fantasy purposes, it largely hasn't mattered because of his red-zone role in this top-10 offense. He's the RB12 on RB4 usage this season, despite being the RB11 in carries and RB23 in receptions.

His fantasy RB1 value is completely tied to scoring touchdowns. Since joining the Cowboys, he's averaged 16.5 points when they've scored 30+ points versus 15.1 in the 20s and 9.4 in the teens.

The good news is the Cowboys project for 28.75 points (second) against a Colts defense that looks lifeless without DT DeForest Buckner. Bhayshul Tuten just finished as the RB2 without playing the fourth quarter, and Chris Rodriguez Jr. was trucking defenders himself. Indianapolis has allowed the sixth-most rushing yards per game this season, and that will probably only get worse.

6. James Cook III (at MIN) - 97.5 proj. total yards

Truly nothing has changed. Cook is the RB4 on RB9 usage this season, averaging 101 rushing YPG (third) on RB41 receptions per game. The Bills offense looked particularly great last week with DJ Moore stretching the field and Dalton Kincaid ripping explosives in multiple TE sets.

The challenge here is the Vikings defense. They just held the Lions to 19 points on the road and are clearly one of the only truly elite defenses. Detroit leaned on the ground game a bit more than usual against them, a trend that's appeared throughout the season against Minnesota. It's allowed the No. 16 rushing YPG versus the No. 31 passing YPG. Opposing offenses have a 45% neutral pass rate against them (second lowest). That said, Jahmyr Gibbs (RB5) and Jonathan Taylor (RB8) both held the line, while mediocre schemes/players have completely busted. Buffalo heads to Minnesota as 3.5-point road favorites with a 25-point implied team total (11th).

7. Bucky Irving (at CHI) - 83.5 proj. total yards

The Bucs are coming off the craziest two-game run script I can remember. Irving has been the RB4 and RB11, respectively in expected half PPR points in them, while Kenny Gainwell has popped off alongside him. Tampa Bay is content running the ball behind its fantastic OL, and while it won't be anywhere near this extreme, I don't see that game plan changing after Baker Mayfield struggled in a pass-first offense early on. Irving's vision and contact balance are a great fit to consistently hit these 20-yard runs. This month, Irving is the RB2 on RB3 usage. He handled the goal-line carry last week, while Sean Tucker looked on from the sideline.

The Bears defense has been stingy against opposing backfields, ranking 29th in half PPR points allowed to running backs at 16.7 per game. Then they added the best DT in Jeffery Simmons. This will be a tough test for an on-fire Bucs run game. The Bears held Bijan Robinson to an RB31 finish in Week 6 and bottled up both Seahawks RBs last week. Tampa Bay heads on the road as 5.5-point road underdogs with an implied team total of 21 points (22nd).

8. Chuba Hubbard (vs. DEN) - 90.5 proj. total yards

Hubbard has been a high-volume anchor, sitting as the RB9 in per-game half PPR points on RB6 usage across his last four contests despite some difficult matchups. He's handled 19.3 carries per game (RB7) over that window, crossing the 20-carry threshold in three of those four outings. In Week 8 against Green Bay, Hubbard set season highs with eight targets and seven receptions, turning that receiving work into 21.4 half PPR points on 24.1 expected points. Jonathon Brooks is starting to practice, but they'll ramp him up slowly and he's unlikely to factor in too much when he is cleared.

The Broncos defense ranks 11th in half PPR points allowed to RBs at 21.9 per game on the season, but their starting lineup is finally back to normal. Opponents have had success through the air against Denver, where the defense allows 8.5 half PPR points per game to receiving RBs (fourth-most in the NFL), highlighted by Emmett Johnson's RB5 finish in Week 8. Carolina's offense also brings distinctive passing tendencies, holding a 62% neutral pass rate that ranks third-highest in the league. The Panthers enter the matchup as 3-point home underdogs with an implied team total of 20.25 points (25th).

9. Josh Jacobs (at NE) - 69.5 proj. total yards

Jacobs was the RB1 overall in usage last week after being the RB5 in expected half PPR points in his debut. He's averaging 18.9 half PPR points on 23.4 expected, essentially trading a couple of runs for a couple of targets in this more pass-heavy Packers offense. That's a great trade-off in fantasy. For context, the Packers had a 46% neutral pass rate in 2024-25 versus a 56% rate this year.

On tape, Jacobs is not in his prime, nor is the Packers offensive line, so he'll definitely be an inefficient player. Green Bay can't trust MarShawn Lloyd in protection or to hit the right hole, so they'll live with whatever Jacobs can produce.

The Patriots rank 26th in half PPR points allowed to opposing running backs, giving up just 17.0 points per game to the position. This was true the year prior, so it's a matchup worth knocking expectations. Recent bell cows D'Andre Swift (RB13) and Braelon Allen (RB19) were just fine. Green Bay heads on the road as 3-point road underdogs with an implied team total of 22.25 points (17th).

The splits with Kyle Monangai are staggering. We'll see if he can get cleared this week. If not, Swift has enjoyed a month-long stretch of being the RB3 on RB2 usage overall.

Tampa Bay has once again been stout against backfields this season, ranking 31st in half PPR points allowed to RBs (14.5 per game). The Buccaneers have been especially tough on the ground, sitting 31st in rushing points allowed per game to the position. Just ask Bijan Robinson (RB28), Jaylen Warren (RB23) and Javonte Williams (RB19) this month. Chicago enters as 5.5-point home favorites with an implied team total of 26.5 points (seventh).

Taylor had his worst game since Week 8 of 2024. That did come against the best fantasy RB defense (Jaguars), but it did also come without LG Quenton Nelson during the middle of Taylor's own trade talks. The Colts been mixing in Seth McGowen earlier in games more than they did before, and their defense is getting blown out, which means Taylor can get scripted out of games. It's nowhere near the same situation as before, dropping him to boom-bust RB2 levels. I anticipate him being far more matchup-elastic given his poor surroundings.

Luckily, the Cowboys are a great spot. They've allowed the third-most fantasy points to RBs on the most rushing yards per game (152.8). It was Tyler Allgeier's turn last week (RB1 overall), but Saquon Barkley looked good on his limited touches the week prior and Bucky Irving dropped 175 yards in Week 5, too. It's bad versus bad on this side of the ball. The Colts have a 22.75-point team total here. It's a much better spot than last week, pending reports that Taylor's role could be changed the week before the trade deadline.

12. Kyren Williams (at WAS) - 84.5 proj. total yards

Williams' role is nearly the same, but the Rams offense hasn't been quite as explosive recently, so his output has slightly turned down. This month, he's the RB17 on RB16 usage, averaging 14.5 carries and 3.0 receptions. He was catching more passes early on, and it's possible the increased usage of 13-personnel is working against his passing role. Blake Corum continues to play every other drive; Corum is an NFL starter on tape to me.

Washington sits 27th in half PPR points allowed to opposing backfields at 17.0 per game on the season, but DT Daron Payne just went to IR. The top-end RBs have played well, including Christian McCaffrey (RB2) and Jonathan Taylor (RB9). Even Saquon Barkley (RB21 in a part-time role) looked good last week. The Rams travel as 5.5-point road favorites, carrying an implied team total of 26.5 points (eighth).

Tuten is a game-time decision with an ankle/foot injury. He hasn't practiced yet but his last play in Week 8 was a touchdown that didn't look painful. This is a good reminder that Chris Rodriguez Jr. is one of the top insurance options in fantasy.

Tuten sits as the RB10 in per-game half PPR scoring on RB18 usage over his last four contests. He has generated efficient production on the ground and through the air, averaging 72 rushing yards (RB13) and 36.3 receiving yards per game (RB7) across that window. Coming off a bye in Week 8, Tuten set season highs with 28.9 half PPR points (RB2) and 104 rushing yards against Indianapolis. The potential for a low-end RB1 finish following that Week 7 bye is pretty clear.

The Ravens present a difficult matchup, ranking 23rd in half PPR points allowed to running backs at 17.3 per game. Baltimore is particularly stout against pass-catching backs, ranking 29th by allowing just 3.7 half PPR receiving points per game. That matches its recent stretch, having limited Chase Brown (RB36) and Bijan Robinson (RB18) to modest days in Weeks 7 and 5, respectively. The Jaguars enter Thursday night as 8.5-point road favorites with an implied total of 25.5 points (ninth).

14. Rhamondre Stevenson (vs. GB) - 86.5 proj. total yards

TreVeyon Henderson (illness) was ruled out early last week and missed Wednesday's practice once again. There are notable splits for Stevenson whether he plays or not. In seven games dating back to last year where Hendo missed or had fewer than five carries, Stevenson is the RB13 on RB16 usage. He drops to RB25 on RB39 usage in the other 19 games. This year, Stevenson is the RB20 on RB28 usage while he's been in and out of the mix. It helps if A.J. Brown and Hunter Henry join Romeo Doubs and Mack Hollins on the sideline, too.

Green Bay represents a favorable rushing matchup, ranking seventh in half PPR points allowed to RBs at 23.1 per game. The Packers sit sixth in points allowed to RB rushing at 16.6 per game, having yielded top-24 fantasy finishes to Chuba Hubbard (RB7), Jahmyr Gibbs (RB18), Javonte Williams (RB17) and D'Andre Swift (RB12) in four consecutive weeks. New England hosts the Packers as 3-point home favorites with an implied team total of 25.25 points (10th).

15. Jonah Coleman (at CAR) - 86.5 proj. total yards

With RJ Harvey (knee) on IR, the backfield was down to two. JK Dobbins started, but just like last week, it was Coleman who ended it. Dobbins was held to just one (one) snap in the second half of Week 8, while Coleman was left wide open for a 51-yard reception and ultimately cut back on a separate carry for a score. He finished as the RB11 on RB6 usage, tallying 11 carries and four receptions. He handled goal-line carries on separate drives, too.

Coleman has a little windup step when buying time as a runner, and he can find some cutback lanes that others won't see. He is a low-built power runner who will try to lower his shoulder for an extra yard. At the same time, he's probably the slowest fantasy starter and did bounce some runs to play into his weakness. He looks like a Day 3 talent who is climbing the depth chart by having fresh legs with some established trust. It's unlikely that Dobbins ever threatens on passing downs, so Coleman is at least the passing-down back with an emerging rushing role. We'll see if the Broncos actually trade for Jonathan Taylor later on. For now, Coleman is an RB2/3.

The upcoming matchup brings a Carolina defense allowing 26.1 half PPR points per game to running backs (second most). The Panthers have been particularly vulnerable on the ground, ranking second in rushing fantasy points allowed to opposing backfields. Recent opposing rushers have capitalized, including Bucky Irving (RB2) and Josh Jacobs (RB10). Denver heads in as 3-point road favorites with an implied team total of 23.25 points (13th).

16. Chase Brown (at ATL) - 73.5 proj. total yards

Brown was the RB20 on RB14 usage last week despite finishing with eight receptions. The good news is Brown still has 8-catch grabs in his pocket and those eventually will lead to more explosives and touchdowns. The problem was he played 2 of 8 snaps inside the 10-yard line, including 1 of 4 inside the 5-yard line. That's where Samaje Perine is mixing in more than normal. Across the month, Brown is the dang RB39 on RB11 usage. He'll settle in as a fantasy RB2 with upside, but the pass game is too good and Brown is too mid to believe in a full top-10 RB1 rebound here.

The matchup with the Falcons is a solid one. They've allowed the sixth-most points to fantasy RBs and were just embarrassed by Bucky Irving and Kenny Gainwell last week. Atlanta has allowed a top-10 fantasy RB in 4 straight outings, and EDGE Samson Ebukam just went to IR. They've missed a LB, too. The Bengals project for 27.75 points (fifth).

17. Ollie Gordon II (vs. DET) - 84.5 proj. total yards

Gordon has taken on significant rushing volume, ranking as the RB16 in half PPR points per game on RB12 usage across his last four outings. Over that stretch, he is averaging 17.8 carries (10th) and 90 rushing yards (eighth), while logging nine carries inside the 5-yard line. His passing-game involvement remains minimal, however, so the risk of being scripted out of the game plan is concerning against great opponents.

The matchup is tough against a Lions defense allowing just 16.2 half PPR points per game to RBs (30th most). Detroit ranks 25th in rushing points allowed and 24th in receiving points allowed to opposing backfields. Miami operates with a run-heavy approach, ranking 30th in neutral pass rate at 44%, while Detroit opponents pass at an extreme 59% clip (third highest). The Dolphins enter as 7-point home underdogs with an implied total of 21.75 points (18th).

Lions LB Jack Campbell will not be out there. It was relieving to hear that he has movement in his lower extremities.

18. Woody Marks (at LAC) - 78.5 proj. total yards

It sounds like David Montgomery is going to miss another one. In three games without him, Marks is the RB29 on RB17 usage, 16.3 carries and 2.7 targets per game. Marks is a replacement-level back who can play in all phases, but expecting efficient outings behind this OL is asking too much. He's finished below his expected half PPR point totals in 5 of 7 games.

The good news is the Chargers' run defense isn't one to fear. They rank 10th in half PPR points allowed to opposing backfields at 22.2 per game. Los Angeles has been particularly vulnerable against ground games, ranking fourth in rushing points allowed per game to running backs (17.3) while ranking 27th against RB receiving. It's allowed a top-16 finish in 4 straight games, including Blake Corum (RB15), Kenneth Walker (RB1), J.K. Dobbins (RB16), and Emanuel Wilson (RB6). Houston enters the contest as 4.5-point road favorites with a 22.5-point implied team total (16th).

19. Saquon Barkley (vs. NYG) - 77.5 proj. total yards

Barkley scored in the red zone on a Wildcat run, showcasing enough speed to get to the pylon. He generally looked just fine on tape, but the Eagles had to bring down his workload again. He's been the RB32 and RB22 in usage over the past two games, and in Week 8, he played just one snap on late downs and 44% of snaps on early downs. It'll be hard to reach top-15 status until he's unbothered by his back and hamstring.

A date with the Giants is neutral, revenge game aside. They're mediocre against the run in general, but both teams can play a run-heavy, slow-paced style that limits fantasy points in general. The Eagles only have an 18.0-point team total; we'll see if anything changes with QB Joe Milton starting over Tanner McKee.

20. Cam Skattebo (at PHI) - 74.5 proj. total yards

Skattebo (hamstring) was working on the side on Wednesday. Make sure Devin Singletary is rostered in your leagues.

The easiest way to parse through Skattebo's rocky 2026 season is to throw out his benching game and include the rest. He's averaged 11.2 half PPR points on 11.4 expected points in the six non-benching games. In Week 7 without Najee Harris, Skattebo was the RB7 (18.6) on RB30 usage (9.1 expected) while easily leading the backfield with 18 touches. Tyrone Tracy Jr. (hamstring) also left that game, potentially leaving Skattebo with Devin Singletary for the short term. He's by far their most powerful rusher, while Tracy and Singletary are change-of-pace players. This is a downhill team that will want to hide J.J. McCarthy as much as possible, so Skattebo is a candidate for 15-25 touches until proven otherwise.

21. Derrick Henry (vs. JAX) - 80.5 proj. total yards

Henry has played seven games without Lamar Jackson over the last three years, and he's averaging 16.8 half PPR points on 17.5 expected points and 22 carries per game. That's the absolute upside case, but the reality is his new baseline is far lower with Tyler Huntley also out and with OC Declan Doyle finding more ways to move the ball than running behind an aging Henry. The last month of the year are a great example of that, when he's been the RB28 on RB17 usage and 17.3 carries per game. That's a better baseline with "Joe Fagnano" under center on Thursday Night Football.

Jacksonville has been the most difficult fantasy RB matchup this year, allowing just 14.2 half PPR points per game to the position on the fewest rushing YPG (77.4). Opposing offenses have the lowest neutral run rate against them, too. It'll be a little easier without DT DaVon Hamilton, but this remains a matchup to avoid. The Ravens only have a 14.75-point team total as 10-point dogs. Henry can get game-scripted out of outings, just as Jonathan Taylor (RB37 on RB20) usage was last week.

22. Blake Corum (at WAS) - 59.5 proj. total yards

Judkins' role is changing for the worse with Dylan Sampson back in the mix. His expected half PPR points have dropped from 13.8 to 11.4 since his return and all of that has come from the receiving game, where his targets' split is 4.0 to 1.5. If the Browns start trailing, Judkins is now at risk of a total dud. He's been the RB22 and RB26 in usage over the last two outings and has been fairly inefficient throughout the year, making him a FLEX rather than a fantasy RB2.

The Saints represent a prime rushing matchup, ranking first in half PPR points allowed to running backs at 27.9 per game. New Orleans has been especially vulnerable on the ground, allowing a league-high 21.2 points per game to RB rushing (first of 32). Over their last four games, the Saints have yielded spike performances to Bijan Robinson (RB5), Brian Robinson (RB8), Aaron Jones (RB7) and Tyrone Tracy (RB8). Cleveland travels as 2.5-point road underdogs with an implied total of 20.5 points (24th).

We'll learn more later this week, but Hall is unlikely to return to his RB12, RB13 and RB33 finishes to start the year with Braelon Allen playing so well in his absence. That's particularly true with the Chiefs on deck. They've allowed the 25th-most rushing yards per game (96.3).

25. Rachaad White (vs. LAR) - 62.5 proj. total yards

Jeremy McNichols (foot) is going to miss months, leaving White and Austin Ekeler at the top of the pecking order with Devin Neal and Craig Reynolds available from the practice squad. White and Ekeler are battling it out in all phases, with White starting last week and finishing off the win with an explosive and goal-line TD late in the fourth quarter. White was the RB17 on RB17 usage last week and was the RB11 on RB22 usage before that. His fantasy low-end RB2 case is pretty straightforward.

The Rams rank 27th in half PPR points allowed per game to opposing backfields (17.0). Their DL is strong enough to overcome a scheme that is daring teams to run. Offenses facing Los Angeles have posted a 47% neutral pass rate, which is the third-lowest rate in the league. Because the Rams are light in the box and teams are often trailing, they have allowed the seventh-most receiving points to RBs, which plays into White's game. Ashton Jeanty was a PPR scam in Week 7 (RB10), and Bam Knight (RB20) and James Cook III (RB4) got home in the weeks before that. Washington is listed as a 5.5-point home underdog with an implied team total of 21.0 points (23rd).

26. Ashton Jeanty (at SF) - 64.5 proj. total yards

Jeanty is the first player to have the same or fewer carries in 8 straight games to open a season, per Doug Clawson on X. I don't think this was pre-game comments or ankle-related either. Coach Klint Kubiak has been scaling down his carries for a long time and communicated to Jeanty before this game of the role change.

Week 8 easily could be a one-game experiment to fix a completely broken run game, but it'd be a surprise if Jeanty went back to his early-season workhorse role. It's clear Kubiak, who pounded the table for Mike Washington Jr. and wasn't around for the Jeanty top-10 selection, views this duo as closer to even than Twitter does. Some of that is scheme-related, as Washington is a better athlete for the wide zone, while Jeanty's power-based skill set is struggling to find cutback lanes and hit the hole in time.

Jeanty's pass-game role is very safe, and I'm currently projecting a more even split on early-down runs, just how it has been from Week 3 on when he's been the RB18 on RB15 usage. The error bars here are obviously extremely wide, but if there's a game script that leans Jeanty, it's clearly while trailing. The Raiders are 10-point dogs to the 49ers.

27. Tyler Allgeier (at SEA) - 66.5 proj. total yards

With Jeremiyah Love (ankle) picking up his third separate high ankle sprain of the season, Allgeier has a chance to consolidate most of the Cardinals' touches. We'll see if Bam Knight (concussion) can get cleared in time. Without him, Allgeier is looking like one of the biggest workhorses of the week. The Cardinals' RB room collectively has averaged 21.5 expected half PPR points per game this year, and if he inherits 80% of that, then he's a top-24 option.

That will be stress-tested against the Seahawks, however. Gardner Minshew will make his first start against a top-2 defense. They've only allowed 94.0 YPG on the ground this year, and they recently kept Kenneth Walker III to 57 and Christian McCaffrey to 83 total yards. D'Andre Swift's 3-score outing on Monday Night Football is absolutely the exception, and the Cardinals unit is nowhere near the Bears'. Arizona has a highly concerning 14.5-point team total.

28. Jadarian Price (vs. ARI) - 55.5 proj. total yards

The backfield is set. Emanuel Wilson was active but didn't play and is on track to be a healthy scratch moving forward, leaving Zach Charbonnet, Jadarian Price and George Holani as the active players. Charbonnet started and played 37% of snaps, leading to 11.6 expected half PPR points (RB22 usage). Price played 48% of snaps, leading to 9.9 expected half PPR points (RB30). Holani played 17%. The trio played on both early and late downs, pass-protected, ran routes and carried the ball.

It's officially a "let-the-best-man-win" committee, for an organization that held back Kenneth Walker III for years. So who looked the best? Take a look at this:

Athletically, it's no contest. Price is so much faster in a straight line and laterally agile. The speed difference is evident on the outside runs, while the lateral ability is easier to see on the cutback runs. That's not the end-all, be all, but it absolutely does matter and it's unlikely to change with Charbonnet coming off a February ACL surgery. Price was the one to receive the pitch on a sweep, so the coaching staff obviously agrees.

That said, Price did have two reps where he probably could've done a lot more with the look. One of them featured him not giving his center the time to climb to the next level. He instead jammed into a crowded lane, where a more patient and crafty runner would've cut back for a nice gain. The other rep was attempting to bounce it outside of nickel CB Kyler Gordon, when he could've hit his inside shoulder hard for a potential nice gain. Neither was egregious, but the top-end veterans treat them differently.

Charbonnet's runs were more textbook, but there was space to carve out real explosives if he was simply faster. Both of the runs in the video above showcase that glaring flaw. I'd understand they always value Charbonnet's size and quality decision-making in short yardage, but the Seahawks would do well if they continued giving Price a lot of the early-down and between-the-20s runs to increase their odds of an explosive and to give Price more time on task to develop.

Arizona ranks 18th in half PPR points allowed to opposing running backs, yielding 19.3 fantasy points per game to the position. The Cardinals sit 19th against RB rushing and 18th against RB receiving, having recently surrendered big finishes like Jahmyr Gibbs (RB1) in Week 5. Seattle enters as 11.5-point home favorites with a 28-point team total (fifth). They didn't have DT Walter Nolen last week and EDGE Josh Sweat is on the trade block.

29. Zach Charbonnet (vs. ARI) - 56.5 proj. total yards

Assuming Travis Etienne Jr. (hamstring) is out again, Miller has been the 1A in the Saints committee. He was the RB9 and QB27 in usage heading into last week's bye, clearing ahead of Alvin Kamara in the high-value touches. Of course, Kamara has actually gotten lucky with the actual production. Miller is the bigger rusher and the favorite for the goal-line plunge.

#2851. JJ Redick challenged the Lakers defensively vs. Heat, felt team’s response was ‘awesome’

Although the Miami Heat are off to a rocky, 2-5 start, they sit a respectable 13th in offensive rating and operate at a breakneck pace. It's a style that could catch any team off-guard — and it often has over the past year or so — especially one playing the second leg of a back-to-back as the Los Angeles Lakers were Monday night. This season, Miami touts the NBA's fastest time to shoot at 10.4 seconds per possession, according to inpredictable, and is fourth in transition frequency (18.4%), per Cleaning the Glass.

In the first half of Monday's duel, the Lakers struggled to corral Miami's uptempo attack. They surrendered 72 points before the break and led by just seven. After intermission, they held the Heat to 45 points and pulled away for a comfortable, 136-118 victory.

"We really challenged the guys at halftime. The biggest thing for us, the first thing on the scouting report was get back and get set," head coach JJ Redick told reporters after the win. "We had multiple clips and multiple guys, after makes, running towards our hoop instead of getting back. This was not a pick up, full-court game. They want to make it a track meet. They want to play high volume early offense."

According to NBA.com, the Lakers posted a 95.7 defensive rating in the second half. Redick attributes much of that success to slowing Miami's early attacks and containing its half-court offense, the former of which featured starkly different performances between halves.

"We gave up 40 points in the first half in the first eight seconds [of a Heat possession]. We gave up, I think, 16 in the second half in the first eight seconds and they were 0.71 [points per possession] in the half court, which is [the 98th percentile], is as good as you can kind of hope for," Redick said. "I thought the way they responded in the second half, or at halftime and then in the second half defensively, was awesome."

Despite being 6-2 with the league's second-ranked offense and fourth-best net rating, the Lakers are just 18th in defensive rating thus far. Moving forward, they'll hope they can parlay Monday's impressive second half into a prolonged stretch of feisty defense to complement their abundantly potent offense.

#2850. Ohio State made the mistakes that the Trojans typically do against USC

On Saturday night, the USC football team upset No. 2 Ohio State 30-18. With the victory, the Trojans improved to 7-2 on the season and 4-2 in Big Ten play.

One of the more notable aspects of the game was that, unlike so many other times in the past under head coach Lincoln Riley, the Trojans did not beat themselves. In one of the biggest games of Riley's tenure, USC played clean, relatively mistake-free football.

Ohio State, on the other hand, committed many of the mistakes that USC has made so often in the past. It was the Buckeyes who had 11 penalties for 71 yards—including several at extremely inopportune times. It was the Buckeyes who dropped multiple passes. It was the Buckeyes who made mistakes on special teams.

This is by no means to take away from what the Trojans did in the game. Plain and simple, USC was the better football team on the field Saturday night.

So many times in the past under Riley, however the Trojans have hurt themselves with self-inflicted errors. This time, however, it was USC's opponent doing so, while the Trojans themselves played relatively clean football. That was certainly nice to see for a change.

#2849. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2847. Harrison Butker is still the Chiefs’ kicker heading into Week 9

Last week, the Kansas City Chiefs brought in former All-Pro kicker Justin Tucker to the practice squad after Harrison Butker's shaky start to the season. "Harrison will be in, yeah," Reid confirmed.

He is 12-for-18 on field goals this year, tied for the lowest rate in the NFL among qualified kickers currently on an active roster; former Los Angeles Rams kicker Harrison Mevis (56%) is the only one with a lower rate.

Butker has attempted the third-most 50-or-more-yard kicks in the league this year (7) but has made only two. Five of his six misses have come from long range, but he has also missed a 45-yard field goal and a point-after attempt through seven games.

On offense, Reid shared that there is also "a good chance" that starting left guard Kingsley Suamataia will be in after being benched (for an unclear reason) in the Week 8 loss to Denver. Veteran backup Mike Caliendo filled in for Suamataia in both games. In his most recent game, Caliendo allowed five pressures — including a sack and two quarterback hits — over 68 snaps at left guard, according to Pro Football Focus. It was also a woeful performance against a Broncos defense that Kansas City ran all over in the season-opening matchup.

With a strong indication that Suamataia will be back in the lineup, it feels like the team knows he is the better player.

So why has a coach's decision kept him off the field in two crucial games this season? It feels like we'll never get that answer.

#2846. The rise of Cooper Flagg: How the Mavericks' teenage star is leading the franchise forward

DALLAS — Not long after an emotional NBA Cup win over the Houston Rockets on Friday, Cooper Flagg was asked about the joys of the Dallas' Mavericks' new two-star dynamic. Flagg and Kyrie Irving had combined for 68 points, 15 assists and 13 rebounds in the contest, the Mavs' third win in a row. But the 19-year-old didn't want to focus on himself or take away from his team's achievement against a legitimate contender.

Flagg redirected the question to preach about players excelling in their various roles regardless of perceived status.

Such a remark can be lost in a postgame interview, especially one where the subject tends to keep things brief and direct. But in that moment, Flagg was displaying a level of leadership that is typically unbeknownst to teenagers, much less budding superstars being handed the keys to a franchise.

(Mallory Bielecki/Yahoo Sports Illustration)

In three months, it will mark two years since the stunning Dallas trade that sent Luka Dončić to Los Angeles in exchange for Anthony Davis. The massive void left by Dončić and the emotional toll from such a jarring move created a butterfly effect for the city and organization, ultimately leading to the drafting of Flagg, but also the firing of head coach Jason Kidd and general manager Nico Harrison, and the hiring of Dusty May and Masai Ujiri this offseason.

But beauty can emerge from despair. So when May and Ujiri embraced in the tunnel after the final buzzer against Houston, hope re-emerged. And although the transition between eras has certainly come with its fair share of detours, Flagg's growing leadership in Year 2 — and, more importantly, his veteran teammates empowering him — has provided a North Star to follow.

"He's taken a step into a leadership role," teammate Daniel Gafford said. "There's different ways you can lead in certain areas. With Coop, he just finds ways to have fun throughout the craziest times. Kyrie [Irving] is one of the biggest leaders in the locker room and he leads by his voice and example. Coop is following suit and taking notes and doing it himself."

"It's huge," Flagg added. "Just getting the respect of my peers and teammates last year was big for me. They're trusting me, I'm trusting myself. It's a good recipe."

Flagg's mental growth has been concurrent with his on-court development. In the shot clock era, there are only 20 players who have averaged 25 points, 5 rebounds, 5 assists, a steal and a block per game. At 19, Flagg is the youngest ever to reach those benchmarks.

Ironically, part of his success stems from the chaos he was born into. As a rookie under Kidd, Flagg was originally miscast as a point guard with questionable spacing at times, and at other junctures shared a backcourt with the likes of Brandon Williams, Ryan Nembhard and D'Angelo Russell — hardly a trio of names that screams development. Flagg also was a victim of Dallas' rotational roulette; only Cleveland and Washington had their most common lineups on the floor together less than Flagg's most consistent one (92 minutes, per NBA.com tracking data) last season.

A year later, Flagg has a clear-cut role in a cleaner half-court setup and has taken lessons from his playmaking past and refined them. His processor has sped up as the game has slowed down for him, better understanding how to attack opposing defenders, crack open schemes and create the best shot for himself and those around him.

As his usage rate has risen, so too has his assist rate, with Flagg now operating in the 98th and 97th percentiles, respectively, per Cleaning the Glass. An increase in possessions as a ball-handler has resulted in top-20 marks in secondary assists, potential assists and assist points created. It's no surprise the Mavericks are 17 points per 100 half-court possessions better with Flagg on the floor.

Flip to a Mavs game on League Pass on a Tuesday night and you'll see Irving receive an inbounds pass, flip it to Flagg and jog to the opposing wing to let him go to work.

"Just comfortable," Flagg said on how he feels with the ball in the half-court. "I'm comfortable getting to my spots and with my teammates. Coach is putting me in great spots."

Dallas was one of the worst spacing units last season, but Flagg now has Irving (a career 40% 3-point shooter) and Max Christie (38%) next to him (and the hope is P.J. Washington returns to his 2024 form, where he shot 38% from deep). May also wants to play with pace and incorporates a high volume of pick-and-rolls to get both Flagg and Irving on the ball. The pace, space and freedom allow Flagg's unorthodox style to take form — more isolations, more midrange attempts and a better overall shot selection. (His 3-point efficiency, hovering around 23%, remains a work in progress, but he's been able to establish elite go-to moves that negate the need to launch unnecessary 3s into the sun.)

Flagg's improved comfort within the offense speaks to his relationship with May; an open, honest one that incorporates feedback and fluidity.

"It's been great," Flagg said of his bond with May. "He watched a ton of film from last year and knows my game pretty well. So just whatever he's seeing where he wants to put me, it's been working really well."

Still, arguably none of this is made possible without Irving's presence, patience and poise. For starters, his gravity alone makes Flagg's job easier on a nightly basis, drawing attention away from him and opening up more space to operate. But Irving's experience, having shared the spotlight with a plethora of big names during his tenure, makes the day-to-day easier as well. For what it's worth, the Mavericks have outscored opponents by 25 points in 98 minutes that Flagg and Irving have been on the floor together. It's been less of a turn-by-turn approach and more cooperative, with Irving wanting to push Flagg to be the best version of himself. As an elder statesman now, it's about giving back to the younger generation.

"It's been great," Irving said to Yahoo Sports. "In my first few years in the league, I had some good veterans — Anthony Parker, Antawn Jamison, Anderson Varejão — they were all good to me. But I was always trying to figure out what more I can do to bring to the team, and the best thing for me was being able to be honest about who I wanted to become in this league and how I was gonna accomplish it. It wasn't a solo job. I think he has a lot more patience than I did when I was his age. With the supporting cast around him, he can trust us with any information or questions he wants to ask."

It takes a village to raise young players. A head coach without NBA experience forced to lean on his roster, creativity and communication. A wily, sage veteran with championship experience that is hell-bent on pouring into the next phase of NBA basketball. A community that supports and sees the full potential of an ascending superstar.

There's still work to do. The Mavericks, despite their winning record, are just 18th in net rating. And it's not just their conference that remains a gauntlet; their own division — with the Rockets, Spurs and Grizzlies ahead of them — is as deep as ever. But with Flagg's continued trajectory, along with the other now-stable pieces in place, Dallas' future is much brighter than its recent past.

"There's always so much room for improvement," Flagg said. "I haven't shot the ball incredibly well behind the arc. But just continuing to play at my own pace, getting better and cleaning up the turnovers. There's always room for improvement."

Quarterback Sam Darnold will not play in Sunday’s game against the Cardinals after suffering a quad injury late in Monday’s loss to the Bears, Seahawks head coach Mike Macdonald announced in his Wednesday press conference.

“It’ll be week-to-week for Sam,” Macdonald said. “He’s not going to go on IR.”

Macdonald noted the club intends to start Drew Lock on Sunday, but he’s also working through an injury.

“In terms of Sam, this guy, he’s tough as nails,” Macdonald said. “I think the second play of the game, I just asked if I could share this, but he cracked his rib on the second play of the game. So, playing the whole game without even making a name of it, and playing his tail off, and competing, it’s worth noting how tough this guy is. He’ll be back sooner than later, I’m sure.”

Darnold has completed 60.2 percent of his passes this season for 1,478 yards with 10 touchdowns and nine interceptions so far this season.

Seattle’s first injury report of the week will be out later on Wednesday.

#2844. NFL Week 9 betting: 5 biggest storylines, including the Bears and Jaguars creeping up Super Bowl odds

The Los Angeles Chargers still haven't done anything for bettors this season.

There are no undefeated teams left in the NFL after the Vikings lost in Week 8, and only one winless team. That's the Chargers. There is also only one team that is undefeated or winless against the spread. That's also the Chargers, who are 0-6-1 according to Action Network's standings. It has been a long season for the Chargers, and anyone backing them.

Here are five betting storylines for Week 9 of the NFL season:

The Chicago Bears had an impressive win over the Seattle Seahawks on Monday night, and now they're one of the top few Super Bowl contenders, according to the odds.

The Bears are +900 to win the Super Bowl, with only two teams ahead of them. The Bills are +550 and the 49ers are +600. The Bears are 6-2, with one of those losses coming when Tyson Bagent started, and just made a major move to add defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons in a trade with the Titans. The offense has been good most of the season (Caleb Williams has moved up to fourth in NFL MVP odds at +800) and the defense looked much different on Monday with Simmons creating chaos in the middle. In a wide-open NFL season, the Bears are making a charge.

Chicago Bears defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons (99) celebrates during the first half of Chicago's win over Seattle. (AP Photo/Mark J. Terrill)

The Jacksonville Jaguars' strong start is getting some notice. Only three teams have better Super Bowl odds at BetMGM than the Jaguars at 10-to-1. The Jaguars and Chiefs are tied for fourth at 10-1. Jacksonville is a heavy -750 to win the AFC South.

The 5-2 Jaguars have been good for most of the season, though they had two questionable games in London. They barely beat the Eagles on the final play and lost to the Texans. But Jacksonville has been pretty good otherwise, leading the NFL with a +85 scoring margin. Jacksonville was 13-4 last season and is once again one of the better teams in the league this season. They're legitimate Super Bowl contenders.

The Jaguars have one of the easier matchups of Week 9, according to the odds. They get to face the reeling Baltimore Ravens, who will be without Lamar Jackson and likely Tyler Huntley on Thursday night. The most likely starting quarterback is undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano, though he's dealing with an ankle injury. Austin Reed, who was signed Monday to the practice squad, has never played in an NFL game and was most recently with the UFL's Dallas Renegades, could be the starter if Fagnano can't play. Not ideal.

If Huntley returns soon, the Ravens won't be massive underdogs often. But they're in a bad spot for Thursday night.

If the Dallas Cowboys can't win on Sunday, the sky really might start falling in Dallas.

The Cowboys are 6-point favorites on the road, which seems hard to believe after they fell to 3-5 with an embarrassing home collapse to the Cardinals. But that is because of this week's opponent: Dallas faces the Indianapolis Colts.

The Colts could trade more players including running back Jonathan Taylor before Sunday. They looked awful in a 42-7 loss last week to the Jaguars, which was their fourth straight loss. The Cowboys probably shouldn't be that big of a road favorite against anyone with how their season is going, but Indianapolis might be the rare team that can't move the ball on Dallas' defense.

Broncos rookie running back Jonah Coleman had 93 yards rushing before Week 8. He had 41 yards rushing on Sunday and now he's second in the NFL Offensive Rookie of the Year race (Coleman did make a big impact with a 51-yard catch to set up his go-ahead touchdown run).

Coleman's +550 odds at BetMGM are behind only Titans receiver Carnell Tate at +375. It hasn't been a great year for offensive rookies. Fernando Mendoza hasn't started a game all season. Jeremiyah Love has been banged up since preseason. Philadelphia's Makai Lemon, the third receiver drafted, got off to a slow start. The second receiver drafted, Jordyn Tyson, hasn't played yet for the Saints due to a hamstring injury. The Jets refuse to feature tight end Kenyon Sadiq, the 16th pick of the draft.

That's how a rookie running back who has missed most of the season due to injury and has 134 rushing yards all season is second in the Rookie of the Year race.

#2843. UTSA-FAU football: How to watch as healthy Roadrunners take winning streak on the road

UTSA safety Elijah Newell acknowledged the adage that nobody is playing at 100% this deep into the season, but the Roadrunners have managed to stay healthier than most.

Nineteen players on offense and 18 on defense have played in all eight games this year, as offensive lineman Ben Rios has been the only regular starter to miss multiple games, sitting out two.

The open date on the schedule ahead of a matchup at FAU at 6:30 p.m. Thursday was almost overkill for UTSA, which has consistently kept a clean bill of health through 2026.

"Probably just all credit to our training staff, strength and conditioning, and our nutrition has all gone way up this year," quarterback Owen McCown said. "Just how they're handling our bodies and the data they're collecting with all of that has been really good."

READ MORE: How rested UTSA aims to stay sharp at Florida Atlantic

Since being promoted to director of sports performance for football in spring 2026, Jared Ka'aiohelo has brought an approach to strength and conditioning that is "just different" from previous years, coach Jeff Traylor said. The program also made a strong commitment to building out the staff, committing "a little bit more money over there to get some better people," Traylor said.

Traylor also credited the athletics training staff, the rest of the sports performance staff, the doctors available to the Roadrunners through the program's collaboration with UT Health, and interim director nutrition Hunter Puestow. UTSA was intentional about investing and changing their practice schedule, Traylor said, and the Roadrunners are seeing the benefits.

Newell said Ka'aiohelo, known to the Roadrunners as "Coach K", put in the effort to learn the needs of each individual player and deliver on their requests.

"It started with the strength staff. They've done a wonderful job with us, really," safety Elijah Newell said. "People come in the morning, do soft tissue work, do all that other. We've been doing that since January. We've really dialed into every little part of the game."

#2841. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."
