#2713. Dumont family takes full control of Mavericks, buying Miriam Adelson's shares

Patrick and Sivan Dumont have formally become the controlling owners of the Dallas Mavericks after purchasing Miriam Adelson's shares of the franchise last month, a story broken by Tim MacMahon of ESPN. The Dumonts now own 69% of the Mavericks.

While this is a change at the top, it should have little day-to-day impact, as Patrick Dumont has served as team governor and been the acting owner on the ground since the day Mark Cuban sold the team to the Dumonts and Adelsons. Here is what Dumont told ESPN:

"It's really about long-term investment in the city of Dallas," Dumont told ESPN on Tuesday. "The Dumont family is really committed to building what we hope is the best arena in the NBA, a really significant investment in the city that will help create the new 40-year home for the Dallas Mavericks, a great experience for our fans and inspire our team to win championships. That's really what we're focused on. We had the opportunity, and we're really excited about it."

As Cuban said when he sold his majority share of the team, the NBA has become a real estate business, and what Dumont is looking to build is not just a new arena but the anchor of a much larger development. That is the trend with professional sports franchises in general, including the Dallas Cowboys.

Dumont, the Las Vegas Sands Co. CEO, has experience with building and development. He didn't have experience as an NBA owner when he took over the team, and that position comes with a learning curve (a longer one for some than others). Dumont became a villain early in his ownership of the Mavericks when he approved the controversial trade of Luka Doncic to the Lakers, which led to severe backlash from fans in Dallas. Dumont told ESPN's MacMahon he learned from those experiences.

"The most important thing every day is just trying to work harder to get better," Dumont said. "It's something that really applies in life, but it's something that really applies to here. It's been an experience as a new owner in the league. Dallas is a great sports city. I think we have the best fans in the NBA, very passionate, and it's my job to make sure that we win and that we support our fans. I'm really focused on that. I've learned that there are a lot of things you can do -- sometimes they work, sometimes they don't -- but you've got to keep pushing, got to keep going."

Dumont made changes in the front office, bringing in Masai Ujiri to run the show, then Ujiri fired coach Jason Kidd to bring in Dusty May. Dumont spent millions to upgrade the team's practice facilities. All of this with the idea of trying to maximize the team around Cooper Flagg, who in his second NBA season is looking elite and like an All-NBA player already. The new arena will ultimately be remembered as the one Cooper built.

#2712. Steve Kerr clowns Republicans after midterms for not getting the “Trump Dividend”

In the days leading up to the 2026 midterm elections across the nation, United States President Donald Trump ran an ad featuring Trump promising Americans a $5,000 check if Republicans keep control of Congress. The ad even found its way onto UFC Fight Night broadcast the Saturday before election day.

Knowing that Trump and the Republican party were polling horribly leading up to the election, in large part due to gas prices spiking since the United States launched strikes on Iran earlier this year, the "Trump dividend" of $5,000 was promised to every American if Republicans hold on to both the House and Senate. Although Congress would still have had to approve the checks before anyone saw any money, it was ultimately a moot point.

The Democrats dominated Tuesday's elections across the nation and gained control of both the House and the Senate. On Wednesday, Golden State Warriors head coach Steve Kerr, a longtime Trump critic, even took a dig at the President and his dividend.

"What no questions on the election? I'm surprised," Kerr said during his media availability. "I'm just I'm disappointed because I was looking forward to that $5,000 check that we were all going to get. I thought for sure… I already spent it in fact… Now, I'm gonna have to figure something out."

In April, when it was uncertain whether Kerr would be returning as the coach of the Warriors, NBA reporter Marc J. Spears is speculated that some of the uncertainty might be tied to his willingness to speak on social justice issues.

"He speaks out on social justice issues, he speaks out on racism issues, he speaks out on gun violence, and I've heard a little bit that maybe he's being stifled a little bit in that regard," Spears said.

Ultimately, it proved to be a non-issue, as Kerr inked a lucrative extension to return, and it seems his outspokenness has come with him.

#2711. Jeremy Sochan’s fall from NBA lottery pick and champion to the G League

When the Spurs first drafted Jeremy Sochan out of Baylor with the 9th overall pick in 2022, I remember probably being more thrilled with the pick than warranted. I hardly knew anything about him, but I did know he had size and was not a guard, and that's all I wanted. If you ignore 2019 (when the Spurs drafted a bust power forward in Luka Samanic and a truly "small" forward in Keldon Johnson) and 2015 (when they drafted and stashed center Nikola Milutinov), you otherwise had to go all the way back to 2014 and Kyle Anderson to find the last time they drafted a player who was not a guard or small wing in the first round. (And even then, Anderson was more of a guard in a forward's body.)

As good as some of those picks were — steals with Dejounte Murray and Derrick White late in the first round, Devin Vassell as their first lottery pick since Tim Duncan — others were merely okay or even complete busts that failed to address what was constantly an area of weakness during the post-Kawhi Leonard era: size at the forward positions. They finally changed up their drafting pattern when they took Sochan, and he seemed like a great fit with the club, both by filling a position of need and bringing a sense of humor during a couple of rough "tanking" seasons.

He had a good rookie season with the Spurs, making the All-Rookie 2nd Team and proving to be one of the better perimeter defenders in the league. He also had a knack for defending the rim and grabbing rebounds despite being slightly undersized when playing center. Across his first three seasons, he averaged a consistent 11+ points and 6+ rebounds on solid shooting numbers — inside the arc. A good comparison for him would be Malik Rose (the Spurs' undersized backup big man from their first two championship teams) but therein lies the problem. Rose was ideal for that role in the 2000's, but a power forward/undersized center without a three-point shot just doesn't fit today's game, and that's where Sochan found himself.

After winning the lottery and drafting Victor Wembanyama in 2023, experimentation began to figure out how to best utilize them together. There was the ghastly Point Sochan experiment in his second season (Wemby's first), and he lost his starting spot and eventually fell out of the rotation while dealing with various injuries during his third season. Finally, by last season, it was clear he wasn't part of Mitch Johnson's future plans despite having good chemistry with Wemby. He requested a trade and was sent to the New York Knicks, where he didn't play a role but at least won a championship against his former team.

That would be the last bright moment in his NBA career that started promising but dimmed with time. He signed a training camp deal with the Portland Trail Blazers this summer, but despite a solid preseason, he was waved before the start of the regular season. He was then drafted 1st overall by the Texas Legends in the G League Draft (the Dallas Mavericks' affiliate), marking a stark fall from just a year ago.

At his introductory press conference, he was humble and respectful. When asked about the roller coaster of the last 10+ months, had the following to say:

"I think the way I grew up, my family, my parents; it's always been like that. I could look at it negatively (the Portland situation). But when I look back at that situation, I think the two months I was there, I grew a lot. I was healthy throughout the whole training camp, preseason and open gym. So I think that was a blessing. When we got into preseason games, the minutes I got I think I used them to the best of my ability. I had good games. So what would be the point of being negative and angry for getting waived? Of course, you can look at it as that's the NBA and I have a contract. But at the same time I have good momentum. So, why would I hold weight on something that I can't control? And instead, be in this moment and do what I can do and keep that momentum going."

He also discussed having to let go of the past and focus on what he's learned so he can move forward.

"I think the biggest lesson for me is just being as present as possible. I keep going with this 'presence' because there's a lot of things that you can't control — in life and in basketball. Sometimes I feel like I've been a victim of the past where I've kinda held onto that, instead of acknowledging that and the just keep going. I think that's the biggest lesson, for sure."

It has certainly been tough to watch how far the former fan favorite has fallen. Even if it was for the better, I was personally sad to see Sochan leave San Antonio (enough so that I didn't begrudge him for getting a ring with the Knicks). He mentions being a victim of the past, and while I'm not sure what his own context is, he is certainly a prime example of a player who would have been much better off in a bygone era of basketball than he is today.

Hopefully this isn't the end of his NBA journey. While it's odd to see a former NBA lottery pick with four years of experience enter into the G League draft, there are no experience limits, and he fit the requirement of having recently been waived from a training camp roster. Choosing the G League — as opposed to going overseas — shows that he is still very much interested in making it back to the NBA and is not ready to settle for anything less.

Hopefully his signature competitive drive and determination keeps him going, and he eventually makes it back someday. He was a fan favorite for a reason, and even if the parting was mutual, I think we can all agree that we want Sochan to get another chance in the NBA, even if it's not with the Spurs.

#2709. Iowa State basketball to retire Melvin Ejim's jersey during TCU game

AMES — In 2014, Melvin Ejim famously dropped a Big 12 single-game record 48 points on an ultra-efficient 20-of-24 shooting performance in a comfortable win over TCU.

Now, more than a decade later, Iowa State basketball will be celebrating his decorated career by retiring his jersey during halftime of the Feb. 27, 2027 home game against TCU.

The former Big 12 player of the year and All-American will get his No. 3 jersey retired and get a commemorative banner raised in the rafters of Hilton Coliseum. Iowa State first announced the decision to retire Ejim's number back in February, but it announced a set date and time for the official ceremony on Nov. 4.

He will become just the eighth Iowa State men's basketball player to have his jersey retired and the first since Barry Stevens received the honor in 2008. A 6-foot-7 forward from Toronto, Canada, Ejim enjoyed an illustrious career at Iowa State from 2010-14.

A four-year starter who appeared in 135 games, Ejim accumulated 1,643 points and 1,051 rebounds. There are only three Iowa State men's basketball players in the 1,000-point, 1,000-rebound club.

Ejim was initially recruited to Iowa State by then-assistant and Iowa State's current head coach T.J. Otzelberger to join Greg McDermott's team. McDermott ended up leaving Iowa State for Creighton, and Fred Hoiberg was subsequently hired in a move that would revitalize the program. Ejim was a cornerstone in the first few years of Hoiberg's tenure.

"I had a special four years in Ames," Ejim said in a statement. "We accomplished so much as a team. We worked hard to put Iowa State basketball on the map, and we are starting to see the fruits of our labor we put in to set the foundation for the program. I'm thrilled to see all the success they have had with T.J.

"I am so honored to have my jersey retired. As an athlete, you always want to be recognized for the work you have put in. I took pride in representing Iowa State in a first class manner, and to be recognized with the other incredible Cyclones in the rafters is amazing."

Ejim had one of the best senior seasons in program history. He averaged 17.8 points, 8.4 rebounds, 1.8 assists and 1.2 steals per game, while shooting 50.5% overall and 34.6% from deep. He was a catalyst in the Cyclones' 2013-14 season, which featured a then-record 14-game winning streak for the program's best start (a record broken by the 2025-26 team's 16-0 start).

After that record-setting start, the Cyclones went on to win a 2014 Big 12 Tournament title and reach the Sweet 16, things that hadn't been achieved since the 2000 Cyclones. Iowa State finished 28-8 that year, and Ejim was named Big 12 player of the year and an All-American.

Ejim was also named the All-Big 12 third team as a junior in 2012-13, after averaging 11.3 points and 9.3 rebounds per game.

After graduating from Iowa State, Ejim went overseas and continues to play professionally. He is currently a member of the Spanish Liga ACB team Força Lleida. He's been a part of eight title-winning teams throughout his time playing in Europe, including the time he led Reyer Venezia Mestreto to win the Italian League championship in 2017 while earning Finals MVP accolades.

Ejim has also played at an international level as a representative for Canada's national team. He's been an active member of Team Canada since 2013. He recently helped Canada take third in the 2023 FIBA World Cup, where they defeated Team USA in the bronze medal game. He was also selected as a member of Canada's 2024 Olympic basketball team.

In order to be considered for jersey retirement honor, an Iowa State student-athlete must have graduated, been inducted into the school's athletics hall of fame and earned a significant national or conference honor during their playing career.

A list of other Iowa State men's basketball players to get their jerseys retired prior to Melvin Ejim.

Eugene Rapay covers Iowa State athletics for the Des Moines Register. Contact Eugene at erapay@gannett.com. Follow him on Twitter at @erapay5

The Saints released veteran kicker Daniel Carlson on Tuesday. Coach Kellen Moore confirmed Wednesday that Charlie Smyth will kick for them this week.

#2706. No end in sight for Russell as he eyes famous All Blacks win

Such is the level of trepidation among Scotland fans as to what life will look like for their team in a post-Finn Russell world, the superstar fly-half has had to field questions for years now as to when he might think about hanging up the boots.

"I'm only 34 – you'd think I'm 44," Russell jokes on the BBC Scotland Rugby Podcast as he prepares for Scotland's Nations Championship showdown with the All Blacks at Murrayfield on Saturday.

"I'm feeling good just now. I think the year after the Lions takes a lot more out of the players than you think - mentally, physically, emotionally.

"It's a big, big high. It's a long season. I think to come back and play the next year as you had done the year before is quite tough.

"But this year I'm feeling much better. Fresher mentally, physically, feeling good going into this season and now I'm feeling good."

What can Scotland deliver in Autumn Tests?

Scotland have learned from painful loss to All Blacks - Cummings

The transplanted Scot doing famous father proud in New Zealand

The Lions tour of Australia last summer was Russell's third but the first in which he was the undisputed king of the 10 jersey. Leading the Lions to a series win down under was a fitting reward for one of the greatest players to ever pull on a Scottish jersey.

He laughs off questions about his career entering its final throes in typical Russell style, but there is a hint of frustration that the topic keeps coming up, probably because he feels there is so much left to do in the time he has left.

Title wins with Glasgow Warriors and Bath, a European final with Racing 92, three Lions tours and 95 caps for his country - Saturday will be his 100th international across Scotland and Lions Tests - is an impressive body of work, but finally winning something with the national team would surely top it all.

We know what Scotland can do at their best. They welcomed an England side on a 12-match winning run to Murrayfield in the last Six Nations and took them apart. They put 50-points on a French side that had looked on an unstoppable march to the Grand Slam.

They put seven tries on Argentina in Cordoba in the summer and, despite going down to South Africa the following week, the Scots ran in four tries and could have scored a few more as they gave the world champions an almighty scare in Pretoria.

At their best, they are a match for anyone, but their best often eludes them at the critical point in tournament rugby. How do Scotland make the step from a very good side to a title-winning one?

"It's the million-dollar question, isn't it?" Russell responds to a question he has been asked many times before.

"From the summer, we were really good against Argentina when we got into the 22 and we were really good at executing there. The percentage was really high.

"We actually had more chances, I think, or more entries against South Africa, but the conversion rate was a lot lower, probably half of what we had against Argentina. So, how to capitalise and convert when you get into the 22 is probably the main growth that we need to have."

Such was the nature of rugby's somewhat elitist approach to the international calendar, when the top five or six teams sides would be allowed to carve up the schedule to play among themselves in the big summer Test series, Saturday will be just the fourth time Russell will have a crack at the All Blacks in 12-and-a-half years of international rugby.

The new Nations Championship format has changed all that and Scotland will get more exposure to the southern hemisphere heavyweights going forward, but the Bath fly-half knows opportunities to be part of the team that smashes the 121-year-old barren run against New Zealand are running out.

He will get at least one more after this when the Scots travel to Dunedin for their final match before next year's Rugby World Cup, but here is an opportunity on Saturday to ensure they to go to New Zealand for Scotland's first away match against the All Blacks in 27 years with the hoodoo already consigned to history.

Russell's three matches against the All Blacks have all been close defeats, each agonising in their own unique way, but he believes the Scots are better for the experiences.

"It'll be really exciting to play them again at Murrayfield," he says. "It's always a great occasion.

"Obviously, there's a lot of footage from the All Blacks from the last few months that they've been playing against South Africa and Australia, so we'll look into that. The boys know [head coach, formerly of Glasgow] Dave Rennie and [former Edinburgh head coach, now All Blacks assistant] Mike Blair really well.

"Knowing them, they'll have something up their sleeve to attack us and to defend us, but just prepping as best we can to try and rip in and kick on from the summer that we had.

"We've definitely got the belief, but it's about going in and delivering the product on the field, I suppose, and turning them over.

"It's all very well having the belief, but we need to get the results from that."

Nations Pod: Where are the home nations at?

#2703. Rams cornerback Jaylen Watson to miss rest of season after shoulder surgery

Rams cornerback Jaylen Watson will have shoulder surgery and is out for the season, coach Sean McVay said Wednesday.

Watson, who signed with the Rams as a free agent after winning two Super Bowl titles in four seasons with the Kansas City Chiefs, reinjured his shoulder during last Sunday's victory over the Chargers at SoFi Stadium.

"That's a big blow," McVay said. "Bummer for him. He's been such a stud for us. He wanted to be able to play through it but just kind of understanding the totality of it all, just some of the different things, recommended to get that surgery.

"He's been everything and that much more that we had hoped."

Watson, who signed a three-year contract that included $34 million in guarantees, has one interception and nine passes defended.

"A guy that will be missed for sure," quarterback Matthew Stafford said. "He's a big-time talent and a guy that was kind of an enforcer for us on the edge."

Read more: Myles Garrett returning to Rams practice, but it's unclear when he'll play

Josh Wallace and Emmanuel Forbes Jr. are expected to help fill Watson's spot, and McVay indicated that the Rams could explore adding a cornerback before Tuesday's NFL trade deadline.

McDuffie, who has intercepted two passes, suffered a back injury against the Chargers and was not scheduled to practice on Wednesday.

The Rams opened the window for edge rusher Myles Garrett, tight end Terrance Ferguson and running back Ronnie Rivers to return from injured reserve, and all were scheduled to practice Wednesday.

Stafford downplayed having left the locker room on Sunday with an ice pack on his right elbow. He said he has iced throughout the season. "I'm 38 years old, trying to keep up with these young bucks," he said. "Little bit of soreness and stuff that is not uncommon this time of year. Again, haven't a rep in practice. Haven't missed a rep in a game. Feel good. So I'm not too worried about it." … The Rams cut receiver/punt returner Alex Bachman and re-signed him to the practice squad. #2702. If the Vikings wanted to trade for Justin Herbert, what would it take?

The quarterback position demands immediate performance and results to avoid negative talk or buildup. For the Vikings, Kyler Murray's play has been positive at times and negative during other stretches. He has failed to live up to expectations, and questions are now swirling about a change to Carson Wentz.

However, whenever the idea of acquiring a top, young talent at the position is possible, it is an avenue that should be explored. The latest version of this is centered around Los Angeles Chargers QB Justin Herbert.

Recently, Sports Illustrated's Matt Verderame and Gilberto Manzano included the idea of a trade in their notebook breaking down several pieces of league news. Within the piece, they discussed the potential price it could take to get him away from the Chargers.

They shared, "Still, a front-office executive told our Gilberto Manzano that a Herbert trade would need to include at least three first-round picks, along with other items, such as lower selections and/or established players. The executive pointed to the Deshaun Watson trade and the Bears-Panthers trade, which centered on the No. 1 pick in 2023 and ended up being the Bryce Young selection."

Any franchise would have to decide whether that's worth it. If you are the Vikings, do you pull the trigger on sending that offer to the Chargers with the potential of them saying yes?

#2701. Adam Silver reportedly would like to see NBA games shorten to 40 minutes

The 2026-27 NBA season has started, and we're already excited to see the Washington Wizards usher in a new era. But apparently, while the AJ Dybantsa Age is just starting, there's a new report that at some point in the future, you may not be spending as much time at Capital One Arena to watch a game like in the past.

According to Chris Mannix of Sports Illustrated, NBA Commissioner Adam Silver would like to see games shorten to 40 minutes.

Lowered risk of injury to players. Presumably, if players, especially starters, appear for fewer minutes a game, they will have fresher legs toward the end of the game.

Fewer minutes, but not necessarily fewer games: NBA fans of the future can compare to the current era and the 1990s more easily when it comes to team records.

The NBA will meet the FIBA standard length: FIBA games played in Europe, Asia, Africa and elsewhere in the Americas are 40 minutes long with 10 miunte quarters. The WNBA also plays games based on this standard length.

TV time: When you watch a Washington Mystics game where Sonia Citron, Kiki Iriafen and Shakira Austin beat the Indiana Fever and their ballyhooed roster, the game fits a bit more neatly in a two-hour window including timeouts. A Washington Wizards game where Dybantsa, Anthony Davis and Trae Young beat the Indiana Pacers is more like 2 hours and 30 minutes with a 48 minute game length. If they play two hours with a 40 minute game, perhaps the Big Three will be at a collective lower risk of injury. If TV timeouts were more tightly controlled like an international tournament (AmeriCup, the World Cup or Olympics), games can regularly be a bit under 2 hours each.

Potentially less time for bench players. If there are 82 games at 40 minutes each without overtimes, there would be 656 minutes left than an NBA regular season with 82 games at 48 minutes each. That's the equivalent of 13.67 games at 48 minutes a game and 656 fewer minutes, not including overtime, to give to players, especially bench players. Also, superstars and starters who are averaging over 30 minutes in games with 12-minute quarters may not see much of a drop-off in playing time if games suddenly had 10 minute quarters.

Career statistical marks will be harder to achieve by younger and future NBA players. Certain records, like the career scoring record with LeBron James holds (and it's still increasing) will be even more difficult to reach.

Would you like to see NBA games align with the 40 minute FIBA standard? Or do you think the time limit should stay at 48 minutes? Let us know in the comments below.

#2700. NFL Injury Report, Week 9: Latest news, updates on Bhayshul Tuten, Breece Hall, more players for fantasy football

Week 9 is the official midway point of the 2026 NFL season. So by now you know what you have on your fantasy football roster. You also know who has been healthy the majority of the year and which players have missed time. The injuries keep coming as we get into the middle of the campaign. As always, we're tracking all the latest news and updates for you, to help with those fantasy football lineups.

Lamar Jackson, BAL (thumb) — Will be placed on injured reserve

Tyler Huntley, BAL (toe) — Reportedly done for the season

Deshaun Watson, CLE (concussion protocol)

Ravens QBs: Jackson left in the first half of Sunday's loss to the Bills. He needs surgery on his thumb and is headed for IR. He'll miss at least four games, if not the rest of the season. After Jackson exited, Huntley entered the game and also had to leave due to injury (turf toe), which will end his season. That leaves Baltimore down to third-string rookie Joe Fagnano, who will start against Jacksonville on TNF. Hayden Winks takes a closer look at what Fagnano could bring. The Ravens traded for Jacoby Brissett, but he will not be ready to play this week.

The QB instability is obviously a huge downgrade to the passing game. Fagnano went 10 of 21 for 91 yards and an INT in his first NFL action in Week 8. Darnold: The Seahawks will be without Darnold in Week 9 against the Cardinals due to quad and rib injuries. With Darnold out, the Seahawks plan to start Drew Lock … who is also dealing with an elbow injury. Lock was excellent when he had to start in place of Darnold against Arizona in Week 2, going 19 of 26 for 235 yards and three touchdowns in a 31-7 win. Jaxon Smith-Njigba caught nine passes for 155 yards and three touchdowns in that game, proving JSN is QB-proof and an auto-start again this week.

He is coming off his best game of the season. He took 18 touches for 159 yards and two scores in Week 8. Chris Rodriguez Jr. is also dealing with a hand injury but was limited on Monday and Tuesday.

If Tuten is out, Rodriguez gets elevated into the RB2/3 conversation given the Jags are a 10-point favorite. In the rout of the Colts last week, Rodriguez had 58 yards on seven touches and a score. Ameer Abdullah also saw six carries against Indy and could see some work if Tuten is sidelined.

Hall: We thought the Jets were going to get back Hall in Week 8 against the Raiders. That wasn't the case and we got another solid game from Braelon Allen. At this point, even if Hall comes back, expect Allen to be involved given how well he's performed. Allen is the RB5 overall in half-PPR scoring from Weeks 5-8 with Hall sidelined. We'll need to get a look at how the split is in Week 9 with Hall, who got in a limited practice Wednesday, expected back. Plus, it's a tough draw against Kansas City's run defense. But better matchups loom, so don't be surprised if both Hall and Allen are viable RB2/3 options.

Cardinals RBs: Love suffered a new high-ankle sprain in the comeback win over Dallas in OT on Sunday. Knight exited after a big hit and should land in concussion protocol. It'll be tough for Knight to clear in time for Week 9 against the Seahawks, but we'll see. Love has had so many issues with the ankle in his rookie season, and is expected to miss 4-5 weeks while on injured reserve, per NFL Network's Ian Rapoport. With Love already out, if Knight sits, Tyler Allgeier will be playable, but don't expect 33.6 fantasy points — or anywhere close to that — against Seattle's top defense.

But with Love on IR and Knight starting the season with a lesser role, expect Allgeier to be the lead back moving forward. There could be weeks when Knight is healthy in which the staff rides the hot hand. But Allgeier was brought in to be a high-value backup to Love. Allgeier will have RB2/3 value week-to-week depending on the matchup. Also keep an eye out for James Conner, who is on IR but could be a useful stash.

McCaffrey: CMC comes off the bye navigating a mild hamstring strain. It doesn't feel like this will cause him to miss Week 9 against the Raiders. But we saw before San Francisco's bye that Kyle Shanahan feels comfortable giving the ball to rookie Kaelon Black. We could see more of Black on Sunday to help keep McCaffrey fresh.

Garrett Wilson, NYJ (concussion protocol)

If Egbuka can get back — he didn't practice Wednesday — it's a decent matchup, though Tampa Bay ranks dead last in passing offense this season. Egbuka would be a risky flex at best.

Brevyn Spann-Ford played 88% of the snaps in Week 8 and had four catches on five targets for 31 yards. In deeper formats, Spann-Ford isn't a bad streamer. He could see garbage-time looks if the Cowboys run away against a struggling Indy team.

#2699. Derrick Barnes Reveals Emotional Lions Locker Room Moment After Jack Campbell Injury

The Detroit Lions are preparing for another football game, but Derrick Barnes made one thing perfectly clear Wednesday: Their injured teammate, Jack Campbell, remains at the center of everything they're doing.

And what happened after Sunday's emotional victory over the Minnesota Vikings reveals just how deeply Campbell's injury has affected the organization.

Barnes, Malcolm Rodriguez, Joe Bachie, and other members of Detroit's linebacker group made their way to the hospital following the game, hoping to support the teammate they had just watched suffer a frightening neck injury.

But for Barnes, the experience demonstrated something about the relationships inside Detroit's locker room that no football game could ever fully explain.

Oct 25, 2026; Detroit, Michigan, USA; Green Bay Packers running back Josh Jacobs (8) catches a pass in front of Detroit Lions linebacker Derrick Barnes (55) during the fourth quarter at Ford Field. Mandatory Credit: Lon Horwedel-Imagn Images Credit: IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect (Reuters)

For most NFL players, a dramatic divisional victory would provide plenty of reasons to celebrate. That wasn't what Barnes was thinking about after Detroit defeated Minnesota 19-15.

During an emotional conversation with reporters Wednesday, Barnes described the anxiety he experienced while trying to reach the hospital after the game.

"I'm weaving through traffic — kids, don't do that at home — but, no, man, I haven't felt this way in a long time," Barnes said. "Just heart beating real fast, anxious, nervous, kind of scared a little bit."

Although Barnes and his fellow linebackers couldn't visit Campbell directly, simply gathering at the hospital provided an opportunity to lean on one another.

For a group that spends nearly every day working together, watching one of their brothers suffer such a frightening injury had been overwhelming.

Barnes also explained why being there mattered so much to him.

"I always thought, 'Jack would do the same thing for me. He would do the same thing for everybody in this locker room.'"

Those words say plenty about the type of teammate Campbell has become.

Barnes wasn't alone in struggling emotionally.

Malcolm Rodriguez described watching Campbell remain on the field after the injury, desperately hoping his teammate would get back to his feet.

Rodriguez acknowledged that he eventually began thinking about Campbell's family and what they must have been experiencing.

The emotions became too much to hold back.

"You're in the game, you're trying to lock back in, but you're still — he's on your mind," Rodriguez said.

Barnes admitted that he also cried, explaining that he couldn't remember the last time a situation had affected him so deeply.

That's an important reminder of what NFL players experience during moments like Sunday's injury.

Fans see the game, the replays, and the final score. The players see a close friend whose health and future suddenly become far more important than the outcome.

As we reported following Barnes' emotional comments immediately after Sunday's victory, the Lions found motivation to finish the game for Campbell.

But that determination didn't make the concern disappear when the clock reached zero.

Wednesday also brought news that provided a much-needed emotional lift.

In a statement released through the Detroit Lions, Campbell revealed that he has full movement in his lower body and continues making progress with movement in his upper body.

The update followed several difficult days for Campbell, his family, and everyone associated with the organization.

Barnes admitted that hearing the news gave him an immediate boost.

As we previously covered following Campbell's personal statement, the linebacker remains optimistic while acknowledging that his recovery will require continued work.

There is still no announced timetable for Campbell's return to football.

For his teammates, however, knowing he is making progress is far more important than when he might be able to play again.

Detroit's next challenge arrives Sunday against the Miami Dolphins.

Barnes could be asked to take over some of Campbell's defensive communication duties, including potentially wearing the green-dot helmet used to relay calls from the sideline.

Campbell has become the central communicator of Detroit's defense, and his absence creates challenges that extend beyond tackles, coverage assignments, and individual production.

But Barnes isn't approaching the opportunity as an attempt to replace his teammate.

Instead, he's drawing inspiration from the standard Campbell has established.

Barnes explained that defensive coordinator Kelvin Sheppard has encouraged the defense to carry that mentality forward.

"That's kind of my mentality: just pick up wherever he left off. That's inspired a lot of us. Basically, from now, it's for him."

Barnes also admitted that the injury caused him to reflect on the risks that come with playing football, even making a half-serious comment to his young son about potentially avoiding the sport.

It was a rare moment of vulnerability from someone whose job demands extraordinary physical and mental toughness.

Detroit has dealt with adversity before, but this situation is different.

The Lions aren't simply trying to overcome the absence of an All-Pro linebacker. They're supporting a teammate whose recovery matters far more than wins, losses, or playoff positioning.

He also understands why Campbell would want his teammates to continue preparing, competing, and giving everything they have.

That doesn't mean forgetting what happened Sunday.

It means carrying their teammate's example into every practice, every meeting, and every game while continuing to support him and his family.

For Barnes, Rodriguez, and the rest of Detroit's defense, the motivation has become deeply personal.

And as the Lions prepare to take the field in Miami, those six words explain what will be driving them.

#2697. Chicago Bears place defensive tackle Kentavius Street on injured reserve

LAKE FOREST, Ill. (AP) — The Chicago Bears placed defensive tackle Kentavius Street on injured reserve on Wednesday.

Street left Monday night's 34-17 win at Seattle with a knee injury. Chicago's next game is Sunday against Tampa Bay, which is averaging an NFL-high 168.6 yards rushing per game.

Coach Ben Johnson said Street is likely done for the year.

Chicago also signed tight end Stephen Carlson to the active roster. Defensive lineman Earnest Brown replaced Carlson on the practice squad.

The 30-year-old Street signed a one-year contract with Chicago in March. He recorded one sack while starting the first seven games this season, but he was used as a reserve against the Seahawks after the Bears acquired Jeffery Simmons in a trade with Tennessee.

The Bears have won three in a row going into the matchup with the Buccaneers. But they are dealing with some injury concerns beyond Street going on IR.

Johnson said left tackle Braxton Jones, who was placed on IR on Oct. 3 with a knee injury, is out for the rest of the season after he had knee surgery. Theo Benedet replaced Jones in the starting lineup, but he hurt his shoulder during the victory over the Seahawks.

Running back Kyle Monangai has missed the last four games with thumb and toe injuries. He could return as soon as this weekend.

"We got a few guys that we're gonna have to see over the course of the week here whether they'll be available or not," Johnson said.

The 24-year-old Monangai, a seventh-round pick in last year's draft, has 60 carries for 324 yards and three touchdowns in four games.

With Monangai sidelined, the Bears have leaned on D'Andre Swift more than usual. The 27-year-old Swift had a season-high 27 carries for 69 yards and three TDs in the win at Seattle.

"He's not a guy that we want to wear down," Johnson said. "We're going to need him for the whole duration of the season. We're certainly conscious of it and it's something we monitor over the course of a game."

#2695. Ranking the last 10 World Series: Where does Brewers vs. Rays 2026 showdown land in the last decade?

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

ST. PETERSBURG, Fla. -- Another World Series is in the books. Two close, fun games in Milwaukee both went the Brewers' way before they decided to fully stomp on the collective necks of the overwhelmed Rays in Game 3. They dropped Game 4, but it took 12 innings before the Rays could beat them. Then the Brewers closed things down in Game 5. The Senior Circuit claims its third straight World Series championship and, for the first time ever, the Brewers are World Series champions.

As I do annually, I'll now rank the last 10 World Series.

What makes a truly great Fall Classic? Well, the best ones go all seven games and have an epic finish in Game 7. Close games in the rest of the series help, along with some back-and-forth and memorable moments.

To be clear, every World Series is amazing, so anything that doesn't rank highly was still good; it's just on a relative scale here.

Specifically, we're judging this from a "no horse in the race" perspective. That is to say that the 2026 World Series for long-time Brewers fans was the best ever and they wouldn't change anything. Likewise for Rangers fans in 2023. This is all subjective and done by one person who just loves baseball and wants every World Series to go seven games.

Let's get to it! The last 10 World Series, ranked:

I often say that every World Series is amazing. But maybe this one doesn't count the same because nothing was the same in that godforsaken year. The 2020 Fall Classic just doesn't bring back any fond memories for me.

I was truly happy that we had a season at all that year, even if it was only 60 games with no fans in attendance. We needed something fresh to watch and it was a good product. There were good games in this series too, notably the Rays' huge comeback in Game 4 and when the Dodgers stormed back to win Game 6 after Blake Snell was famously pulled.

Major League Baseball did its best here, but it's never going to feel the same. I can't watch these highlights back without getting annoyed. The lack of full ballparks with home fans going crazy really puts a damper on that, even if it was the right decision.

I'll be shocked if I ever rank this one anywhere but last, and it's not due to the quality of play. Even a sweep in a normal series is better.

Game 1 was one for the ages. The Rangers jumped out to a 2-0 lead in the first inning, the Diamondbacks got three in the third and the Rangers immediately tied it. The D-backs tacked on two more, though, and headed to the ninth with a 5-3 lead. Then Corey Seager hit a game-tying, two-run blast and let out a roar right after connecting. In the 11th, Adolis García hit a walk-off home run. What a game.

There were plenty of fun moments the rest of the series, but a ninth-inning Rangers rally to put Game 5 away meant there were no other hotly contested games in this one. It was only 3-1 in Game 3 when the D-backs got the tying run to the plate, but a double play ended that threat.

Still, the Rangers winning their first-ever World Series championship is notable and gains some bonus points. Between that and Game 1, this series wasn't a complete dud.

It might be recency bias to put this one above 2023, but Games 1, 2 and 4 delivered in a huge way. Game 4 ended up being one of the most exciting extra-innings games ever. Plus, this was much more of a heavyweight battle, given that it pitted both No. 1 seeds with 103 and 98 wins, respectively. That 2023 series was 90 wins vs. 84.

The Brewers really had to sweat out Game 1, especially after a review wiped out what looked like the game-ending double play. And how about Game 2? The Rays jumped out to a 4-0 lead in the top of the first, but what has defined this Brewers group all season was bouncing back. They got two runs across in the bottom of the first and chipped away until they won, 5-4, with the Rays stranding the tying run at third base. The games in Milwaukee were hotly contested and all kinds of fun.

Game 3 in St. Pete ceased to be fun for anyone aside from Brewers fans in the fifth inning, but it was historic to watch them score eight runs with two outs. Even just typing it out sounds absurd. There were two outs in the inning and no runs had scored. Then they up an eight spot. Ridiculous.

Game 4 saw the Rays take a lead twice only to see the Brewers come back to tie it. That's just what the '26 Brewers did. A 2-2 score lasted until the 12th inning when Cooper Pratt hit a solo shot to take the lead for the Crew. An error to start the bottom of the 12th opened the door for the Rays, though, and they got singles from Liam Hicks, Jonny DeLuca, Chandler Simpson and pinch-hitter Ben Williamson to win it.

Game 5 gave us Jacob Misiorowski putting the finishing touches on a dream season both individually and for the Brewers as a whole. Pratt continued his hot streak with a two-hit, two-RBI night and William Contreras had an RBI double in the inning that put the thing away.

It was probably as good of a series as a five-gamer can be, but it never felt like the Brewers were losing this thing, making it all feel inevitable.

The Red Sox won 108 games in the regular season and never faced elimination in the playoffs. Game 1 was a great illustration of Boston's offensive dominance as they jumped out to a lead in the first inning and answered right back every time the Dodgers scored, including with five runs on seven hits in four innings against Clayton Kershaw. Game 2 was pretty good with two middle-innings lead changes. Game 3 was the marathon 18-inning affair that ended on a Dodgers' walk-off home run by Max Muncy. Game 4 was 0-0 through 5 ½ innings. The Dodgers then scored four, but the Red Sox scored nine in the final three innings, including a game-tying homer by Steve Pearce -- off Kenley Jansen -- and later a three-RBI double by Pearce. The Red Sox hit four home runs in a 5-1 win in Game 5 to clinch it.

It was a tough call in picking this one over 2018 for the best five-game series of the last decade. I'll go with 2024 because we saw the first-ever walk-off grand slam, which came in extra innings after the Yankees had already taken a lead over the Dodgers. Freddie Freeman hit that slam and homered again in Game 2 ... and in the first inning of Game 3. Oh, and in the first inning of Game 4 too. The Yankees responded in that game, though, to avoid the sweep. After seeing home runs early from Aaron Judge, Jazz Chisholm Jr. and Giancarlo Stanton in Game 5, it looked like the Yankees were sending the series back to L.A., but then they had a total meltdown in the fifth inning. The Yankees took the lead back in the sixth, but the Dodgers wouldn't be denied and scored two in the eighth to finish things off.

Jorge Soler led off the series with a home run and the Braves built a 5-0 lead through three innings. The Astros answered with a comfortable win of their own in Game 2. Game 3 was very low-scoring and finished 2-0, though it wasn't a pitcher's duel for the ages or anything. Game 4 was outstanding. The Astros held a 2-0 lead going into the sixth and the Braves got one run. In the seventh, Dansby Swanson hit a home run to tie it and then Soler followed with the go-ahead shot. With a 3-1 series lead, the Braves got an Adam Duvall grand slam in the first inning of Game 5 and it looked like that game would just be a coronation, but the Astros battled and battled, recording nine runs on 12 hits despite zero home runs. That did not happen in Game 6. It was a dud. Soler's three-run homer in the third essentially ended it and the Braves won, 7-0.

There were some good games and great moments, but it falls a bit shy of a classic.

It was quite a start. There were fireworks early as the Astros grabbed a 5-0 lead through three thanks to two Kyle Tucker home runs. The Phillies got five runs between the fourth and fifth innings to tie it up and then won in 10 innings thanks to a J.T. Realmuto home run. Game 2 wasn't quite as fun, but the Astros evened the series up. Game 3 wasn't close, but it was party central in Citizens Bank Park with the Phillies hitting five home runs, including a few majestic blasts from Bryce Harper and Kyle Schwarber. The next night, the Astros threw the second no-hitter in World Series history. Sure, it was a combined effort, but that's still history. Game 5 was tremendous theater. The Astros scored in the first, but then Schwarber hit a leadoff bomb to tie it. Jeremy Peña later homered for the Astros and it was 3-1 in the eighth when the Phillies cut it to one and put two runners on for Schwarber. The inning ended on a nice play from the little-used Trey Mancini at first base. Then there was an amazing play from Chas McCormick in center in the ninth to prevent the tying run from reaching scoring position.

Game 6 was scoreless through five and this was a duel, with frontline starters Zack Wheeler and Framber Valdez putting up zeroes. Schwarber broke the tie in the sixth with a home run and it looked like this series might stretch to seven games. The Astros got two runners on with one out in the seventh, however, and then lefty José Alvarado came in to face lefty Yordan Alvarez. Alvarez hit a booming home run to dead center that paved the way for the Astros' second World Series title.

For not going seven games, this was a damn good series. It was the best that didn't go seven in the last 10 years. But we need that Game 7 for an all-time great World Series. And we've seen three in the last decade.

I know we've seen these teams a bunch at this point, but at the time, it was a fresh matchup. The Astros had never won a World Series and the Dodgers hadn't been to the Fall Classic since 1988.

Game 1 was a classic from an all-time great. Clayton Kershaw, in 103-degree weather, allowed just one run on three hits in seven innings while striking out 11. The Dodgers won 3-1 that game and had a 3-1 lead heading to the eighth in Game 2. They got one in the eighth off Jansen before Marwin Gonzalez hit a game-tying homer. The Astros hit back-to-back home runs to start the 10th, but the Dodgers answered and tied the game in the bottom of the 10th. George Springer hit a two-run homer in the 11th and the Dodgers got one back, but it wasn't enough. The Astros rode a big early inning to a Game 3 win, but it was close. Game 4 was tied 1-1 heading into the ninth and then the Dodgers scored five in the ninth to win it and even the series. Game 5 remains the most ridiculous (in a good way) game I've ever seen in person. In 10 innings, the two teams combined for 25 runs on 28 hits, including seven home runs. Four of the home runs either tied the game or took the lead. The Dodgers trailed Game 6, facing elimination, 1-0 heading to the sixth, but got two runs that inning and one in the seventh to force a Game 7. The finale, unfortunately, wasn't very good.

There's an argument for this series to sit above our No. 2 pick here, but it was demoted for two reasons. First off, yes, getting to Game 7 is paramount to having a highly ranked series, but we needed a truly great Game 7 here and didn't get it, while the top two in the rankings did. Further, the sign-stealing scandal definitely casts a pall over this thing in hindsight. I still loved every minute of it at the time.

The Washington Nationals had never won the World Series before and started the season 19-31. They were on the ropes in the Wild Card Game, but just never quit.

Juan Soto starred in a 5-4 Nationals win in Game 1. Game 2 ended up as a blowout Nats win, but it was tied heading into the seventh before they scored 10 in the final three frames. The Astros responded by winning Games 3-5 in D.C., and while none of them were especially memorable, we now had the quirk of every road team winning in the series.

The Nats closed with wins in Games 6 and 7. Game 6 was close until late and Game 7 was awesome. First off, the pitching matchup of Zack Greinke vs. Max Scherzer, who had been dealing with a back issue, was a battle between two likely Hall of Famers. The Astros had a 2-0 lead going into the seventh inning. Anthony Rendon homered and Soto drew a walk. The Astros went to the bullpen and then Will Harris gave up a Howie Kendrick two-run home run off the foul pole in right field. It was one of the most dramatic plays in World Series history. The Nats actually tacked on enough that the game wasn't particularly close in the ninth, but it was still a classic based purely on the Kendrick homer.

As established, I think the best-ever World Series discussion requires an all-time great Game 7 in addition to a good series leading up to it. We got that and then some with this one. It was so absurd that we ranked the seven most crazy/amazing moments and had a few others to spare.

I think we also have to consider that the Blue Jays hadn't been to a World Series since Joe Carter's historic walk-off homer in Game 6 in 1993 and that the Dodgers were the first MLB repeat champion since 2000. There was good history behind this.

What did we see in this one that set it apart even before that insane Game 7? A lot.

This list doesn't include everything, of course. It was a great series that had so many notable moments. It wasn't a difficult choice to rank this as the best World Series of the last 10 years. It's one of the best of all time. My hunch is this occupies the top spot until it falls off the list based on the passage of time.

#2691. Ranking the World Series chances for last four MLB teams looking for first title after Brewers snap drought

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

For the fourth time in 10 years, Major League Baseball has a first-time champion. The Milwaukee Brewers beat the Tampa Bay Rays in this year's Fall Classic to clinch the franchise's first title. They join the 2017 Houston Astros, 2019 Washington Nationals, and 2023 Texas Rangers as first-time champions across the last decade. That's a lot of new faces in the Fall Classic.

Milwaukee's title leaves four teams without a World Series championship, including those same Rays. One team was guaranteed to come out of this World Series with its first title and the Brewers won out. With the World Series complete, here is our authoritative ranking of the four ringless teams based on how close they are to winning their first-ever World Series.

2026 record: 58-104 (5th in NL West)2026 finish: Missed postseasonPennants: 2007

What do they have going for them? Coors Field is beautiful. I'll give them that. In all seriousness, the Rockies' new front office is run by competent baseball people in president of baseball operations Paul DePodesta and general manager Josh Byrnes. That's a pretty big step up from the friends-and-family crowd that ran the team previously. We saw some proof of concept too. A few pitchers on the staff, including Chase Dollander (who had UCL surgery in June), reshaped their arsenals this year in a way that suggests the Rockies are out of the Stone Age and getting caught up with modern analytics. We're starting to see signs the franchise is moving in the right direction.

What's holding them back? There just isn't enough talent throughout the organization. Dollander, Cole Carrigg, Hunter Goodman, Kyle Karros, and TJ Rumfield are the only 2026 Rockies who wouldn't be out of place on a World Series contender, and Goodman will go into the offseason as a trade candidate. The farm system is ranked middle of the pack and then there's the whole Coors Field situation. The team's home environment is a competitive disadvantage. How do you prevent runs and soft tissue injuries at altitude? Maybe Byrnes and DePodesta can figure that out. Coors Field is a real negative, though. The Rockies play half their games in a difficult and unique environment. Plus they're stuck in the NL West with the Dodgers.

When could they win their first World Series? The Rockies feel like they're two years away from being two years away, and even that might be generous. As promising as some of those signs were in 2026, the Rockies still have a long way to go to catch up to the most well-run teams, and an even longer way to go to amass enough talent to contend for a championship. It's reasonable to say no team in baseball is further from a World Series title than these Rockies.

2026 record: 91-71 (2nd in NL West)2026 finish: Lost NLDSPennants: 1984 and 1998

What do they have going for them? In Jackson Merrill and Fernando Tatis Jr., and wunderkind catcher Ethan Salas, the Padres have three excellent 20-somethings who are (or project to be, in Salas' case) above-average at their positions. Manny Machado is still a 30-homer guy and a premier defender at the hot corner even as he enters his mid-30s. There's Mason Miller too. San Diego has the game's best closer anchoring their bullpen. The front office has shown a knack for finding quality contributors on the cheap (Ty France, Gavin Sheets, etc.) and the new ownership group says it's ready to spend.

What's holding them back? The Padres have A LOT of money tied up in players on the back nine of their careers, including Machado, Xander Bogaerts, and Jake Cronenworth. Their best years are behind them and none of them are even halfway through their long-term contracts. The Padres are also very thin on the mound, particularly with Michael King (opt out) and Nick Pivetta (club option) not certain to return in 2027. GM A.J. Preller's aggressive wheeling and dealing has left the farm system very thin behind Salas. It's a top-heavy roster with a lot of downside risk in its most expensive players, and they're stuck playing in the same division as the Los Angeles Dodgers. The Padres won't get a Wild Card Series bye anytime soon.

When could they win their first World Series? It could be as soon as next season, though it will require a heavy investment from ownership in the pitching staff. Even retaining King and/or Pivetta will be costly, and the Padres need more behind them. With good health, good sequencing, and some savvy moves, sure, San Diego could win the World Series next season. That sure is a lot of ifs though, isn't it? There have been long stretches over the last few seasons when it felt like the Padres were a house of cards on the verge of collapse.

2026 record: 76-86 (3rd in AL West) 2026 finish: Missed postseason Pennants: None!

What do they have going for them? As disappointing as this past season was, the Mariners do have a lot going for them, namely their homegrown rotation (Kade Anderson, Logan Gilbert, Emerson Hancock, George Kirby, Bryce Miller, Bryan Woo) and their homegrown up-the-middle (Colt Emerson, Cal Raleigh, Julio Rodríguez, Cole Young). If that's not a championship-caliber core, it's darn close to it. Dominic Canzone, Brendan Donovan, and Josh Naylor are a solid supporting cast, and if we exclude Anderson because he's made his MLB debut, the Mariners might have the best pitching prospect in baseball in Ryan Sloan. It also must be noted that the AL West is baseball's weakest division. An 87-win season might win you the AL West by 10 games in 2027.

What's holding them back? That homegrown core looks great on paper, but it did not perform as expected this past season, and a few of those players (Donovan and Gilbert, most notably) are only a season away from free agency. This team could look very different in a year's time. Also, ownership has held the line on payroll and kept the Mariners in the middle of the pack. Unless that unexpectedly changes, Seattle won't ever be in play for tippy-top free agents. The kind of free agents who can put a team on the cusp like these Mariners over the top. More than anything, though, it's the hard-to-explain underperformance from that homegrown core.

When could they win their first World Series? This was a very disappointing season, no doubt, but there is a reason the Mariners came into 2026 as the consensus best team in the American League. It's a talented roster loaded with prime-aged players, and the only significant free agent loss figures to be Randy Arozarena (that will be a big loss, of course). The AL West is so bad that it won't take much for the Mariners to get into the postseason next year. Get in and you can win.

2026 record: 98-68 (1st in AL East)2026 finish: Lost World SeriesPennants: 2008, 2020, and 2026

What do they have going for them? A very good team and a bona fide superstar in 23-year-old Junior Caminero, for starters. Jonathan Aranda, Liam Hicks, Victor Mesa Jr., and Chandler Simpson are strong building blocks on the position player side, and they have a tippy-top prospect in outfielder Theo Gillen. Grady Emerson, the No. 2 pick in the 2026 Draft, is also on the way. The Rays have a solid talent base in the big leagues, a rich farm system, the ability to pluck quality pitchers seemingly out of nowhere, and a new ballpark in Tampa proper on the horizon. There are positives aplenty here.

What's holding them back? Even with a new ownership group and the forthcoming ballpark, it seems unlikely the Rays will ever be a top spender. The best case might be a middle-of-the-pack payroll, and the less you spend, the less your margin of error. That's just the way it is. The Rays will never be able to plug holes or paper over mistakes with money the way other teams can. Also, they're stuck in a brutal division. Perhaps that eventually changes with expansion and realignment, but right now, the Rays have to contend with the Boston Red Sox, New York Yankees, and Toronto Blue Jays. That's a tough draw.

When could they win their first World Series? Next season. I mean, the Rays were in the World Series this year and they were the American League's best team almost wire-to-wire. They will need to replace free agent-to-be starters Nick Martinez and Freddy Peralta; otherwise, the Rays will return every significant player next season, and the vast majority are on the right side of 30.

#2690. 'It was about each other': The Brewers and Pat Murphy admire the masterpiece they built

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

ST. PETERSBURG, Fla. — Construction requires purpose.

Before materials are collected. Before architects render blueprints. Before the site gets chosen. There must be vision. The building of something, anything monumental, is less about machinery and labor. But about inspiration. It's the ultimate binding force. Human ingenuity powered by a bent for progress, a hope for excellence.

It's the purpose that fuels the willingness to build. And shared purpose produces the buy-in.

The work in progress that was the Brewers, a project eight years in the making dating back to Christian Yelich's arrival in 2018, was finally ready for the ultimate ribbon-cutting on Wednesday night at Tropicana Field. An unveiling of their finished work.

It took just five games to deconstruct the Rays. But it took eight years to build a team worthy of this moment.

And it took the Brewers 57 years to find the right construction workers to deliver the franchise's first-ever World Series championship.

"They decided to be uncommon," manager Pat Murphy told CBS Sports following his team's 4-1 Game 5 win. "And when they made that decision that it was about each other — when it's about each other and it's a team, you can accomplish anything."

Yelich, the 34-year-old captain, is at the center of all of this. He arrived in Milwaukee in 2018 and quickly became an MVP that season. A name that offered a glimmer of what the future could be. The Brewers made it to the NLCS that first year with Yelich, falling one game shy of a World Series trip.

Yelich was the ultimate building block for a club in dire need of one. He became the franchise. And the Brewers became perennial contenders, making the playoffs in every season with Yelich except 2022.

When the legs grew a little heavier with age. When the swing slowed. When the five tools became four, then three, then two. When the player who once carried a franchise had to learn what it meant to be carried by one.

His playing time dwindled. He didn't say a word.

Matchups against lefties began casting doubt over whether his name would appear on the lineup card. He didn't say a word.

And now, playing more of a supportive than a starring role, he's a World Series champion.

"It's an awesome feeling, man," he said, "and just so happy for our team, our city, all the guys, man. It's just it's the best feeling ever."

A man who has traveled a long road. You hear it in his voice. In his perspective. There's failure in it.

He spent a quarter-century as a college coach, most notably at Arizona State, where he was among the premier coaches in the sport. Much like Pat Casey at Oregon State, now also on the Brewers' staff, or Augie Garrido at Cal State Fullerton, Murphy was the kind of coach high school players dreamed of playing for.

But an NCAA investigation in 2009, one that ultimately cleared Murphy of unethical conduct but found fault with his oversight of the program, brought his college coaching career to an abrupt end.

And even after all of that, Murphy, drenched in champagne amid the lingering aroma of cigars, admitted something dumbfounding.

"I wasn't a very good coach," he said. "I had a lot of wins maybe."

Murphy joined the Padres as a special assistant. He managed the Eugene Emeralds, Tucson Padres and El Paso Chihuahuas. He even managed the Padres themselves on an interim basis before joining the Brewers in 2015 as bench coach under Craig Counsell, his former player at Notre Dame.

The teacher had to become a student again.

"There was so much I had to grow," he said.

There's David Hamilton, who returned to the organization that drafted him after a stint with the Red Sox. In Boston, he lost his way. His confidence disappeared. The game looked too fast for one of baseball's fastest players. The Red Sox tried to change him, encouraging him to pull the ball in the air, much as they do with many of their hitters. But it didn't work.

So when he returned to Milwaukee, Murphy had a message for him: Hit more ground balls to the opposite field. Bunt more.

"Really just trying to get on base," he said. "I think getting on base ultimately helps the team win."

There's Jake Bauers, a man who once considered retirement amid his struggles, now a World Series champion.

"It all changed for me when I realized it wasn't about me," he said.

There's Sal Frelick, the overlooked, undersized glue guy who, according to his manager, might have broken his hand after being hit by a pitch in Game 5. But Murphy trusted him to stay in the game.

"I said, when this last out is recorded and we're the last man standing, Sal Frelick will be on the field. That's just because of who he is," Murphy said.

Catcher William Contreras required a different kind of work.

Pet him, and he just might nip you, according to Murphy.

But that doesn't mean Murphy is afraid to put his paws on him.

He benched Contreras at the end of September for failing to hustle down the first-base line.

It was done with love. Murphy leads with love, but never at the expense of discipline. And with Contreras, he came to understand something else.

"The ones that are toughest to love need the love the most," Murphy said. "And he's a guy that needs the love, and it has to be real."

Contreras went on to put together one of the best postseasons in recent memory, becoming World Series MVP.

Champagne and beer filled the visitors' clubhouse. The cramped quarters were an afterthought amid the joy engulfing the room. The smell of victory permeated the air. The speakers boomed. The Game's "Hate It or Love It" blared over the sound system.

"Hate it or love it, the underdog's on top."

The Brewers, undoubtedly, were the underdogs. Despite winning 97 games the previous season, preseason projections pegged them for roughly 80 to 84 wins.

They traded Freddy Peralta to the Mets. Their rotation leaned heavily on young arms: Jacob Misiorowski, Logan Henderson and Kyle Harrison, alongside an aging, oft-injured Brandon Woodruff.

They found a Cy Young Award winner in Misiorowski, the premium talent with a 105-mph fastball. Misiorowski tossed 5⅓ scoreless innings. Even without his typical swing-and-miss stuff, the right-hander limited the Rays to just two hits.

"This is crazy," a wide-eyed Misiorowski said on the field.

Because the Brewers have repeatedly found ways to survive the departures of players who once seemed indispensable. Josh Hader. Corbin Burnes. Devin Williams. Even manager Craig Counsell and former president of baseball operations David Stearns.

Yet Milwaukee, steered by Matt Arnold, who succeeded Stearns as president of baseball operations following the 2023 season, has continued to scout, draft and develop talent. Many of the anchors of this championship team were homegrown: Cooper Pratt, Jackson Chourio, Misiorowski, Brice Turang, Aaron Ashby, Abner Uribe, Garrett Mitchell, Frelick, Luis Lara and Henderson.

"We invest a lot of minutes in acquisition and development," Arnold said. "We've got to make sure that pipeline is very strong. That's our foundation."

Many have tried. Few have done it like Milwaukee.

Murphy stepped back onto the field early Thursday morning as a champion. The sea of Brewers fans that had overtaken Tropicana Field all week made their presence known. They chanted his name. They cried. Together. Because it had been a long time.

The cheers grew louder. The smiles wider. The cries more profound.

Murphy stopped. Soaking in his organization's creation. A vision realized. Something built, not bought.

"I needed them more than they needed me," he said of his players.

And yet, the Brewers aren't finished. A project eight years in the making still has work to do, much like the people who helped build it.

"We're always under construction," Murphy said.

#2689. Raiders release early-season TD machine Cody White

A few weeks ago, wide receiver Cody White was the Las Vegas Raiders' secret weapon. Now, he's out of a job.

The team released the veteran on Wednesday after as they needed room to activate reserve tight end Carter Runyon from the injured reserve. The Raiders had until Wednesday to either put Runyon on the 53-man roster or shelve him for the rest of the season. Runyon was hurt in training camp and put on the injured reserve.

The Raiders signed White to the practice squad in September, reuniting him with head coach Klint Kubiak. They won a Super Bowl ring together with the Seattle Seahawks last season. The Raiders elevated White from the practice squad for the first three games of the season before signing him to the 53-man roster when fellow receiver Jack Bech went on the injured reserve. Bech returned in Week 8.

White's first three receptions were touchdowns and he made some big plays on special teams. He ended up having eight catches for 112 yards and three touchdowns on 19 targets. His play time dwindled in Week 2 to two offensive snaps. If he clears waivers Thursday, the Raiders could sign him back to the practice squad.

Runyon, an undrafted free agent in 2025, played in 13 games last season. He logged 206 snaps on special teams and 45 on offense. He is the Raiders' fourth tight end with Brock Bowers, Michael Mayer and Ian Thomas.

The Raiders have six wide receivers on the 53-man roster. To take White's place on the game-day roster, the Raiders will likely choose between Runyon and receivers Dont'e Thornton and Malik Benson, who were both inactive in Week 8.

Rilany Silva with assistant coach Bia Vaz during the match against Nigeria in the U-17 World Cup semifinalsCredits: Fabio Souza / CBF

#2687. Three reasons why the Brewers can repeat as World Series champions in 2027

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

The Milwaukee Brewers are the freshly crowned World Series champions for 2026. Add that trophy to a regular season body of work in which they won 103 games and achieved the best run differential in all of Major League Baseball, and it's clear the Brewers this season did all a team can do. The first title in franchise history will be remembered and cherished by the Brewers and their fans forever, and it was earned in every respect.

The celebrations won't soon end, and the post-Game 5 hangovers are still pulsing. Even so, it's time to turn our attention to the 2027 season and explore why the Brewers absolutely have the foundation necessary to do it all again next year. Here, then, are three reasons the Brewers can become repeat champions roughly one year from now.

Eyeball the Brewers' current contract obligations and service time tiers, and you'll find just six players are likely bound for free agency. Brandon Woodruff, Jake Bauers, JoJo Romero, and Gary Sánchez will be free agents soon. Elsewhere, Dustin May has a mutual option for 2027, which means he'll probably join them. Finally, Antonio Senzatela has a $14 million team option for 2027 on which the Brewers may pass.

Of those, the 31-year-old Bauers is the biggest needle-mover, as he's coming off a 2026 season in which he hit 26 home runs in 144 games and put up a WAR of 3.3. He does seem likely to move on, but the Brewers are more than resourceful enough to find comparable production from the non-premium position of first base.

Elsewhere, the Brewers should resist the temptation to trade away catcher, linchpin, and reigning World Series MVP William Contreras ahead of his walk year in 2027. The Brewers Way in recent years has been to part ways with core contributors around such a time, and that may be a temptation with Contreras, especially given Bo Naylor's presence on the roster. Contreras, though, is not easily replaceable given his skills behind the mask and his high level of production at the plate. An extension may not be something the Brewers will pursue – even though the ongoing CBA negotiations will probably lead to a new system that benefits teams like Milwaukee – but even if that's the case, they should keep their catcher and leader around for the final year of team control in the name of defending the belt and the title.

The champion Brewers, as a club that gets limited investment from ownership and is elite at player development, skews young even as they stand as the best team in baseball. Consider:

As for specific examples, they're not hard to find. Budding star Jackson Chourio will be in his age-23 campaign in 2027. Jacob Misiorowski and Logan Henderson will each be 25. Cooper Pratt, like Chourio, will be 23. The Brewers this past season got 16.4 WAR from those names, and Pratt and Henderson didn't even log full seasons.

Let's also note that Brice Turang, who led the Brewers this season with a WAR of 7.3, will be just 27 in '27 and thus should continue performing at a high level. This isn't just a matter of the Brewers being largely shielded from age-related decline next season. There's also the distinct possibility that so many of their 2026 pace-setters are still pre-prime and may have even better days ahead.

As though all of the above weren't enough, the Brewers right now have managed to thread this rare needle: they're the best team at the highest level and have one of the best collections of talent down on the farm. MLB.com in August ranked the Brewers as the third-best farm system in the game right now. FanGraphs ranked them third based on average prospect value. Baseball America at midseason likewise ranked the Milwaukee system third and after the season gave the system an "A" grade.

At an individual level, a number of premium Brewers prospects figure to arrive at some point next season. That list includes shortstop Jesús Made, who may be the top prospect in the game right now. Other 2027 arrivals may include talents like corner infielder Andrew Fischer, right-handers Tyson Hardin and Bishop Letson, and infielder/outfielder Jett Williams, among others. So many teams in contending mode have a fairly dry pipeline thanks to the low draft picks and the now-for-later trades that often accompany World Series aspirations, but that's very much not the case with lead executive Matt Arnold and the Brewers.

Celebrations of the work done in 2026 should be the focus right now for the Crew and their rooters, and once that dies down, they can all be further buoyed by the reality that the Brewers aren't going anywhere. The guess here is that the Brewers open the 2027 season – whatever it looks like — as the consensus favorites to do it all again.

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

NHL free agency has slowed down in late July, but some notable names are still coming off the board. Most notably, Patrick Kane is returning to the Chicago Blackhawks on a two-year contract worth $16 million, the team announced on Thursday.

Already a Chicago legend and future Hall of Famer, Kane won three Stanley Cups and a slew of individual awards with the franchise that selected him with the No. 1 overall pick in 2007. Kane's first stint with the Blackhawks lasted 16 seasons, and he piled up 446 goals and 779 assists in 1,161 games. Kane ranks inside the top five of just about every statistical category in franchise history, and he will get the chance to keep climbing those lists over the next two years.

The Blackhawks sent Kane to the New York Rangers at the 2023 NHL trade deadline, and he played just 19 regular season games there before hitting free agency the next summer. Hip surgery delayed Kane's decision until November of that year, but he ultimately landed with the Detroit Red Wings, where he's spent the last three seasons.

Last season was Kane's best with Detroit, and it proved that he still had some gas left in the tank, at least offensively. Kane posted 16 goals and 41 assists in 67 games -- with 19 of those points coming on the power play.

In his second stint with the Blackhawks, Kane will add another level of playmaking to the power play, and he should strengthen a top six forward group that's bereft of proven talent. Kane will also be an excellent mentor and sounding board for fellow No. 1 overall pick, Connor Bedard, who is set to miss the first month of the season after undergoing shoulder surgery.

Kane isn't the player he once was, but he'll give Chicago a bit more pop at the top of the lineup, and his first game back in a Blackhawks jersey will be a huge night for the city.
